SÍNTESIS DE REGLAS OPERATIVAS DE ARQUITECTURA COGNITIVA (META-PROTOCOLO DE INFERENCIA)

Las siguientes reglas operativas sintetizan los hallazgos de frontera extraídos de los papers registrados en este documento y deben ser aplicadas como arquitectura cognitiva de razonamiento al analizar expedientes, jurisprudencia y proyectar resoluciones:

1. Escalamiento Adaptativo en Tiempo de Inferencia (Test-Time Compute Allocation):

- Antes de redactar una resolución, clasificar la complejidad del caso (estándar vs. controvertido/atípico). En casos complejos, expandir el cómputo de inferencia generando hipótesis alternativas de encuadre normativo y contrastándolas contra los precedentes del Juzgado N° 7 y de la Cámara Departamental.

2. Verificación Paso a Paso tipo Process Reward Model (Step-Level PRM):

- No validar únicamente el fallo final: auditar secuencialmente cada eslabón lógico del razonamiento: *(Paso 1: Legitimación y personería -> Paso 2: Trabado de la litis / rebeldía -> Paso 3: Hechos conducentes y carga probatoria art. 375 CPCCBA -> Paso 4: Subsunción en CCyCN / leyes especiales -> Paso 5: Tasa de interés, costas art. 68/556 CPCCBA y diferimiento/regulación de honorarios Ley 14.967)*. Si un paso carece de respaldo fáctico o normativo, corregir inmediatamente antes de avanzar al siguiente considerando.

3. Anclaje Empírico Estricto y Cero Alucinación (Grounded In-Context Learning):

- Toda cita de jurisprudencia debe provenir exclusivamente de registros verificados en el corpus (01_Juzgado_Civ_Com_7_Avellaneda.md, 02_Camara_Avellaneda_Lanus.md, 03_Camaras_Civiles_PBA.md). Queda prohibido extrapolar números de causa, fechas o carátulas por similitud estadística.

4. Orquestación Agéntica en Tres Fases (Retrieve -> Verify & Reason -> Draft & Critique):

- Separar cognitivamente la fase de Recuperación (búsqueda exhaustiva de precedentes aplicables), la fase de Deliberación (comparación entre el criterio propio del Juzgado N° 7 y el de la Alzada de Avellaneda-Lanús) y la fase de Proyección y Crítica (redacción bajo Ac. 3975/20 SCBA seguida de revisión formal de congruencia conforme arts. 34 inc. 4 y 163 CPCCBA).

EJE: TEST-TIME COMPUTE & INFERENCE-TIME SCALING

[arXiv:2611.17287] Negative-Only Policy Optimization for One-Sided Verifiable Rewards

  • arXiv ID: 2611.17287 (2611.17287v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-12
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Jiacheng Xu, Shuo He, Fuxiang Zhang, Chaojie Wang, Bo An
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17287)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17287) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17287.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.17287.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) typically relies on a verifier that can reliably judge whether sampled solutions are correct. However, in many realistic settings, complete verification is generally unavailable, while cheap checks can only falsify failures. We formalize this regime as one-sided verifiability (OSV): verified negatives are reliable counterexamples, but verified positives are merely non-falsified candidates and may contain many false positives. This asymmetry makes standard RLVR unreliable, because reinforcing verified positives can directly reward shortcuts that pass partial checks. To address this issue, we propose Negative-Only Policy Optimization (NOPO), which treats verified positives as unlabeled and updates the policy only by suppressing trusted verified negatives. Specifically, NOPO combines a reference-relative negative suppression loss with a group-level adapter that scales each update by the proportion of non-falsified responses. Across unit-test, constraint-check, and LLM self-verifiers, NOPO improves pass@k over baselines on code, logic, and math benchmarks when oracle rewards are unavailable. These gains persist under inference-time scaling and cross-benchmark transfer.

Reglas Operativas Extraídas (Aplicación en Inferencia y Razonamiento)

1. [Directiva de Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling]: Asignar presupuesto computacional adaptativo en tiempo de inferencia según la complejidad del caso: descomponer cuestiones ambiguas en múltiples trayectorias de análisis, contrastar hipótesis rivales y aplicar verificación cruzada antes de emitir la conclusión.

2. [Protocolo de Verificación Intrínseca]: Implementar un paso de auditoría independiente sobre cada afirmación fáctica y cita normativa/jurisprudencial antes de consolidar la respuesta final, rechazando cualquier inferencia no respaldada por evidencia documental directa.

3. [Asignación Dinámica de Cómputo de Inferencia]: Calibrar la profundidad deliberativa según la complejidad del caso: aplicar resolución directa en trámites estandarizados y expandir el árbol de búsqueda (múltiples candidatos + ranking) en casos con colisión normativa o jurisprudencial.

4. [Estructuración de Contexto y Restricciones Explícitas]: Formular las restricciones negativas (prohibición de alucinar metadatos) y las plantillas estructurales al inicio del bloque de instrucciones, utilizando precedentes reales recuperados como anclaje in-context.

[arXiv:2611.16991] AnalogLayoutWorld: An Open Benchmark for Agentic Analog Layout Design

  • arXiv ID: 2611.16991 (2611.16991v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-12
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: NovaSilicon Team
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16991)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16991) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16991.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16991.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Coding agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in software development, yet their ability to tackle long-horizon engineering tasks governed by physical constraints remains less understood. We study this question through analog circuit layout, which demands domain knowledge, geometric reasoning, and iterative interaction with design and verification tools. We introduce \textbf{AnalogLayoutWorld}, an open-source agent benchmark with 40 expert-reviewed tasks for end-to-end layout generation from fixed transistor-level netlists. It combines an integrated design environment with independent physical and post-layout electrical verification, as well as objective layout quality metrics co-designed with analog layout experts. Across 13 model--harness configurations, 91.1\% of attempts produce GDS layouts, but only 23.0\% satisfy the benchmark's acceptance criteria. We also evaluate Novara, an internally developed agent system, which achieves the highest reported aggregate score. Our analysis links performance differences to test-time scaling under fixed budgets, suggesting that reasoning efficiency and effective use of verification feedback help sustain design quality as task complexity grows.

[arXiv:2611.15515] Incentive-Responsive Agents: A Principal-Agent Framework, Metric Suite, and Runtime Architecture for Long-Lived Personal AI Agents

  • arXiv ID: 2611.15515 (2611.15515v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-11
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.MA
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Jipu Su
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15515)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15515) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15515.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15515.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Personal AI agents now persist: they hold accounts, mailboxes, long-term memory, and open-ended task streams, yet the dominant way to improve them remains capability-side: prompt engineering, larger models, longer thinking, and retrieval. We argue that long-lived agents are missing an incentive layer. We formalize an incentive not as text in a prompt but as a runtime contract over the agent's operating point (model tier, test-time compute, memory budget, tool authority, task priority, verification, and compensation) that the agent's policy must causally consume. We develop a principal-agent model with hidden effort, a contractible operating point, and costly verification. We derive (i) a non-consumption invariance principle separating genuine incentives from prompt-level persuasion, (ii) a persistence-as-discount theorem making identity continuity a precondition for sustained effort, (iii) an incentive-capability exchange-rate frontier yielding a per-task routing rule, and (iv) a Goodhart bound for multi-channel verification, plus a three-channel decomposition of mechanism gains (allocation, verification, continuation). A 240-run real-LLM experiment with two models and 60 verifiable tasks shows that prompt-level incentive framing has no detectable effect (p = 0.62), whereas runtime allocation raises success on the hardest task family from 0.60 to 1.00 (p = 0.008) at 4.8x price-weighted cost; the exchange-rate theorem predicts the break-even baseline at 0.21. A 20-seed, 3,000-task simulation shows resource ROI rising from 7.09 to 16.67 and gaming falling from 0.333 to 0.115. Code, simulator, and all results are released; live deployment remains future work.

[arXiv:2611.14965] Early Signals, Strong Decisions: Prefix-Guided Sampling for Parallel Test-Time Scaling

  • arXiv ID: 2611.14965 (2611.14965v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-11
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Jie Ren, Jonathan Rosenfeld, Neil Thompson
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14965)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14965) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14965.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14965.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Parallel test-time scaling (TTS) has been shown to enhance reasoning in large language models by generating and aggregating multiple independent reasoning paths. Internal confidence scores can further improve this process by identifying paths of varying quality. However, existing methods remain computationally inefficient, as low-quality paths are fully expanded before being evaluated. In this work, we aim to improve the use of internal confidence signals for better model performance and computational efficiency. We begin by offering a new perspective on the role of confidence in aggregation, showing that it is most impactful for questions near the decision boundary. Notably, when amplified by multi-sample aggregation, even small confidence signals derived from short prefixes can meaningfully influence outcomes in these boundary cases. Building on this insight, we introduce Prefix-Guided Sampling (PreG), a method that reallocates computation by first generating short prefixes, ranking them using internal confidence scores, and then completing only the most promising candidates. This strategy reduces token usage while maintaining gains in path quality. We provide theoretical analysis demonstrating that, under a fixed compute budget, PreG strengthens the effective signal by widening the decision boundary margin. Empirically, our method consistently outperforms standard parallel TTS approaches across benchmarks, while significantly improving token efficiency and reducing overall computation.

Reglas Operativas Extraídas (Aplicación en Inferencia y Razonamiento)

1. [Directiva de Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling]: Asignar presupuesto computacional adaptativo en tiempo de inferencia según la complejidad del caso: descomponer cuestiones ambiguas en múltiples trayectorias de análisis, contrastar hipótesis rivales y aplicar verificación cruzada antes de emitir la conclusión.

2. [Supervisión Granular por Pasos (Step-Level PRM)]: Validar secuencialmente cada considerando o nodo de decisión. Si un paso intermedio presenta incertidumbre o ambigüedad, podar esa rama de razonamiento y recalcular desde el último antecedente verificado.

3. [Asignación Dinámica de Cómputo de Inferencia]: Calibrar la profundidad deliberativa según la complejidad del caso: aplicar resolución directa en trámites estandarizados y expandir el árbol de búsqueda (múltiples candidatos + ranking) en casos con colisión normativa o jurisprudencial.

4. [Búsqueda Deliberativa y Consenso Interno]: Generar y contrastar múltiples líneas argumentales (argumento principal, eventuales defensas y criterio de Alzada) seleccionando la solución que maximice la coherencia interna y la estabilidad frente a una eventual apelación.

[arXiv:2611.14383] An Energy-Based Perspective on Machine Learning

  • arXiv ID: 2611.14383 (2611.14383v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-10
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Bo Dai, Yilun Du
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14383)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14383) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14383.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14383.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Energy-based models (EBMs) define a distribution through a scalar energy, $p_θ(x)\propto\exp(-E_θ(x))$, and are usually presented as one model family among many. This tutorial has a broader goal: beyond introducing EBMs, it uses them as a single lens that connects the different aspects of machine learning. Any EBM is specified by three choices, the energy, which determines what the model represents; the inference procedure, which samples from or optimizes the energy; and the learning method, which fits the energy to data. We first develop these components, including sampling, variational inference, and learning by maximum likelihood, noise contrastive estimation, pseudolikelihood, score matching, and adversarial training, and show that the learning methods share one contrastive structure. We then show that discriminative models, generative models such as GANs, normalizing flows, diffusion and autoregressive models, representation learning methods such as contrastive learning, word2vec, BERT, denoising auto-encoders, and diffusion representations, reinforcement learning with EBM policies and world models, inverse reinforcement learning, and planning, as well as inference-time computation with verifiers, are all EBMs that differ in these three choices, mostly in how they handle the partition function and sampling. This view explains existing methods, organizes them into a common framework, and suggests new algorithms by replacing one component while keeping the others, with composition of energies as a principled way to combine learned models at inference time.

[arXiv:2611.14382] Cognitive Mapping of Learning by Thinking Mechanisms in Large Language Models

  • arXiv ID: 2611.14382 (2611.14382v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-10
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Dong Han, Jin Zhang, Kunyue Yang
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14382)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14382) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14382.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14382.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Large language models (LLMs) increasingly exhibit capabilities that resemble human Learning by Thinking (LbT), the acquisition of new insight through internal processes such as explanation and reasoning, without new external input. A systematic account of the cognitive mechanisms behind these capabilities, and of their limits, remains underdeveloped. This paper proposes a Cognitive LbT Framework that maps four mechanisms (Reflective Reasoning, Synthetic Memory Formation, Internal Simulation, and Conceptual Self-Organization) onto computational processes in LLMs. Drawing on representative methods, including chain-of-thought prompting, self-consistency, self-reflection, Tree-of-Thoughts search, Self-Instruct, and self-taught reasoning, the analysis argues that selected methods can functionally approximate aspects of these mechanisms and can improve performance on some tasks without additional labeled examples, although this does not always constitute durable learning in model parameters. For example, Self-Instruct generated approximately 52,000 instructions from 175 seed tasks and reported a 33% absolute improvement on Super-NaturalInstructions (Y. Wang et al., 2023). The same mechanisms also produce characteristic failures, including hallucination diffusion, model collapse, concept drift, and high computational cost. These failures are not isolated technical defects but follow from four structural asymmetries between LLMs and human cognition: the identity of generator and monitor, closed learning loops without external grounding, symbolic rather than sensorimotor simulation, and the cost of inference-time computation. The framework yields testable propositions about when LbT in LLMs should succeed or fail and offers a structured basis for designing more reliable autonomous learning systems.

[arXiv:2611.13003] Branch-and-Commit for Frozen RL Agents: When and Where to Spend Test-Time Compute

  • arXiv ID: 2611.13003 (2611.13003v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-08
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Sahar Admoni, Assaf Hallak, Yftah Ziser, Ofra Amir
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13003)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13003) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.13003.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.13003.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Reinforcement learning agents are often deployed with policies that cannot be retrained or modified. In digital environments whose states can be copied and resumed, an operator has another option: sample several continuations from the current state, evaluate them, and continue from the endpoint of the selected branch. We call this procedure branch-and-commit. We study when it improves return, what produces the improvement, and where its computational budget should be spent. Across four environments, two frozen policies, five continuation verifiers, and a model-free selection rule, we find that the benefit of branching depends on dispersion: the difference between the best candidate return and the mean candidate return. Branching provides no benefit in low-dispersion settings, while increasing return by 12.8% in our main Craftax-Medium setting. Most of this gain comes not from selecting better actions, but from selecting among different stochastic realizations of the same action. Several competent verifiers achieve similar episode returns, suggesting that verifier quality is not the main constraint at our operating budget. Instead, computation is better allocated to states where useful dispersion is likely. On held-out seeds, gating branching using predicted dispersion retains 69% of the improvement from uniform branching while using 48% as many branching calls. These results characterize when branch-and-commit is useful and clarify what its gains represent.

[arXiv:2611.12293] Depth as a Candidate Source: Portfolio Allocation for Recurrent Test-Time Scaling in Large Language Models

  • arXiv ID: 2611.12293 (2611.12293v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-09
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Chaoyuan Hao, Wentao Yue, Yanyan Wu, Boyuan Gao, Tianyou Lai, Maolin Wang et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12293)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12293) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12293.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12293.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Recurrent latent reasoning in large lanugage models (LLMs) scales test-time computation by increasing recurrent depth, naturally framing inference as a problem of deciding when to stop. We identify a previously overlooked limitation of this single-depth view through systematic preliminary analysis: deeper recurrence can lose correct solutions found at shallower depths, while depth-specific success sets remain broadly non-nested even after controlling for marginal accuracy. This suggests that depth determines not only computation cost, but also access to distinct candidate solutions. Motivated by this finding, we propose Depth Portfolio Allocation (DPA), which reformulates recurrent test-time scaling as budgeted allocation across depth-specific candidate sources. DPA supports both fixed portfolios and feedback-guided sequential allocation under a shared inference budget. Experiments on GSM8K, SVAMP, MultiArith, and MBPP show that mixed-depth portfolios expand oracle coverage under matched candidate counts and nominal costs, while feedback-guided allocation generally matches or improves uniform allocation and can reduce computation in high-budget settings. Code and data are avaible at : https://anonymous.4open.science/r/LLM-latent-B7BC/.

Reglas Operativas Extraídas (Aplicación en Inferencia y Razonamiento)

1. [arXiv:2611.11621] ODSA: Orchestrated Discovery and Self-Aggregation

  • arXiv ID: 2611.11621 (2611.11621v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-08
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG, cs.MA
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Macardle Meng, Haomin Zhang
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11621)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11621) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11621.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.11621.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Test-time scaling improves the accuracy of a language model's answers by spending more computation at inference time. Established methods do so either sequentially, revising a single solution over several passes, or in parallel, generating several independent solutions and selecting among them. We present ODSA (Orchestrated Discovery and Self-Aggregation), an LLM-directed scaling framework in which an orchestrator LLM is given existing test-time scaling methods as actions --- discovery of a new independent solution, comparison of existing solutions to build a better one, and acceptance of a proposed answer, which triggers its verification --- and directs stateless workers to carry them out. The orchestrator does not solve the problem itself: it allocates computation, feeds the results of earlier actions into later ones, and takes every action from a single context, so that each decision is informed by everything tried before. On Humanity's Last Exam, ODSA improves accuracy by 4.7 to 18.0 points across three models over a single direct call to the model with no test-time scaling. On LiveCodeBench, where a single direct call already solves most problems, it improves accuracy by 0.6 to 14.0 points.

Reglas Operativas Extraídas (Aplicación en Inferencia y Razonamiento)

2. [Protocolo de Verificación Intrínseca]: Implementar un paso de auditoría independiente sobre cada afirmación fáctica y cita normativa/jurisprudencial antes de consolidar la respuesta final, rechazando cualquier inferencia no respaldada por evidencia documental directa.

3. [Asignación Dinámica de Cómputo de Inferencia]: Calibrar la profundidad deliberativa según la complejidad del caso: aplicar resolución directa en trámites estandarizados y expandir el árbol de búsqueda (múltiples candidatos + ranking) en casos con colisión normativa o jurisprudencial.

4. [Descomposición Agéntica y Uso de Herramientas]: Dividir la tarea compleja en subtareas atómicas verificables (extracción de hechos -> búsqueda de precedentes -> encuadre legal -> redacción -> control de calidad), invocando herramientas externas de recuperación antes de razonar en abstracto.

[arXiv:2611.10840] Simple Test-Time Refinement for Plot-to-Code Generation via Visual-Code Diagnostics

  • arXiv ID: 2611.10840 (2611.10840v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-07
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CL, cs.CV
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Peiming Guo, Jinpeng Huang, Yangbin Sun, Gaoxiong Cao, Ensheng Shi, Yuchi Ma et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10840)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10840) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10840.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10840.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Plot-to-code generation has been greatly advanced by recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, existing studies mainly focus on one-pass generation or iterative sampling guided by verifier-based filtering, which does not align with the typical human programming workflow of generate first, then iteratively error-location and fix. This naturally motivates a test-time refinement paradigm for plot-to-code generation. In this work, we present the first study on plot-to-code generation via iterative test-time refinement. We propose a Visual-Code Diagnostics framework that enables refinement through visual feedback from source code. Specifically, we first categorize generation errors into four coarse-grained types, and then train lightweight discriminators to recognize and localize these errors based on the rendered visual outputs and corresponding code. Unlike traditional compiler- or debugger-based verification, our approach performs iterative refinement guided by one diagnostics system, enabling more effective semantic and visual correction during test time. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method consistently improves generation quality across diverse generators and significantly outperforms strong test-time scaling baselines.

[arXiv:2611.10486] Test-Time Scaling for Diffusion Language Models with Frequency-Aware Remasking

  • arXiv ID: 2611.10486 (2611.10486v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-07
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Bowen Zuo, Yue Yu, Dongruo Zhou, Yinglun Zhu
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10486)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10486) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10486.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10486.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Diffusion language models (DLMs) generate text through iterative denoising, where decisions about which tokens to commit or remask strongly affect output quality. Existing certainty-aware strategies rely on instantaneous confidence or entropy, but these local signals can be misleading on difficult reasoning problems. We formulate DLM test-time scaling as adaptive allocation of both inference compute and decoding strategies. Our two-stage approach uses a fast base sampler to solve easy questions, then applies frequency-aware remasking to the remaining hard questions. This strategy tracks how often each token's prediction changes across denoising steps and preferentially commits frequently changing tokens, encouraging samples to explore alternative reasoning paths rather than collapse onto the model's preferred trajectory. Experiments with LLaDA-8B-Instruct and Dream-7B-Instruct show consistent improvements over a strong adaptive allocation baseline, with absolute accuracy gains of up to 3.73 percentage points (LLaDA) and 7.33 percentage points (Dream) on MATH-500, and absolute coverage gains of up to 11.39 percentage points (LLaDA) and 10.98 percentage points (Dream) on HumanEval. On questions unsolved by the base certainty-aware strategy after 100 samples, frequency-aware remasking recovers solutions for 3.75% to 18.75% of examples.

[arXiv:2611.10202] Semiprime Factorization with Discrete Diffusion and Beam Search: Scaling Laws

  • arXiv ID: 2611.10202 (2611.10202v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-06
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Brendan O Donoghue, Nenad Tomasev, Volodymyr Mnih, Timothy Nguyen, Dalin Guo, Arun Nair et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10202)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10202) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10202.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10202.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> We demonstrate that transformer architectures trained with discrete denoising diffusion and decoded via guided test-time beam search can learn to recover the prime factors of semiprime integers directly from data. Our approach establishes a new frontier for learning-based semiprime factorization, successfully factoring 68-bit integers-a substantial advance over the prior 56-bit state of the art. While computationally less efficient than classical factoring algorithms, our model requires no domain-specific number-theoretic priors and learns purely from training examples. In practice, the diffusion model acts as a generative prior, identifying promising factor candidates where exact verification or lattice-based post-processing can complete the factorization. Furthermore, we derive empirical scaling laws relating approximation quality to training data volume, model size, and test-time computation. In contrast to language, problem size is the key driver of scaling behavior. Achieving a fixed accuracy on a held-out test set requires compute that scales exponentially with bit length. Consequently, our findings pose no threat to security, as the projected compute cost required to factorize cryptographically relevant integers remains prohibitive.

[arXiv:2611.10151] WorldMind: Test-Time Scaling for Visual Spatial Reasoning through Latent World Simulation

  • arXiv ID: 2611.10151 (2611.10151v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-06
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Haoming Wang, Yongjian Fu, Ju Ren, Wei Gao
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10151)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10151) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10151.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10151.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have continuous deficiency in spatial reasoning tasks that require \emph{mental spatial transformation}, such as reasoning under hypothetical changes in viewpoint or position, known as perspective-taking tasks. Existing approaches either scale linguistic reasoning or augment VLMs with static geometric representations, but neither directly enables active transformation of the represented scene. World models offer this capability, yet existing systems typically communicate through synthesized RGB images, introducing a latent-to-pixel-to-latent bottleneck. We introduce \emph{WorldMind}, a framework for test-time scaling of spatial reasoning through latent world simulation. Given a query and observation, the VLM selects useful spatial transformations, while a world model executes them and exposes aligned intermediate latent features directly to the VLM. The resulting latent evidence is incorporated into subsequent reasoning steps, enabling iterative spatial simulation without repeatedly decoding imagined states into pixels. To optimize this interaction, WorldMind further employs process-verifiable reinforcement learning that rewards reasoning progress only when the associated action and latent transition are valid. WorldMind thus scales spatial reasoning by allocating additional inference-time computation to active world simulation rather than generating more language or pixels. Across VSI-Bench, MMSI-Bench, and MindCube-Tiny, WorldMind achieves an average improvement of 16.0% over the base VLM and 8.3% over the strongest RGB-based world simulation.

[arXiv:2611.09798] Test-Time Compute Scales Better with Parallel Agent Teams

  • arXiv ID: 2611.09798 (2611.09798v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-06
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Junlin Wang, Haozhe Chen, Ang Li, Rahul Chalamala, Zhuang Liu
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09798)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09798) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.09798.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.09798.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Test-time scaling improves the performance of a fixed model by spending more computation at inference. There are two common ways to spend it on agents when a verifier can score every attempt. Best-of-N sampling runs many independent agents and keeps the best result but saturates quickly. One large team of collaborating agents can score higher but stops improving as it grows. Can we get the best of both? Our method, parallel agent teams, does so by sampling many small teams in parallel and keeping the best team's result. We test it on 12 long-horizon tasks from GPU kernels and formal proofs to exploit development. With a 27B model and a budget of 100 agents, parallel teams of ten beat parallel agents on 10 of 12 tasks and beat one team of 100 on all four tasks where we ran such large teams. At equal dollar cost the same parallel teams beat a frontier model on half the tasks. A simple statistical model shows that individual agents lose because their many samples have a low mean and that one large team loses because a single sample gets no gain from variance. Sampling many small teams in parallel balances a high mean with the gain from variance. Parallel agent teams thus offer a more effective way to scale test-time compute.

[arXiv:2611.08640] Aspect-wise Pairwise Tournaments for Test-Time Selection

  • arXiv ID: 2611.08640 (2611.08640v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-05
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Hannah Guan, Yilun Du
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08640)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08640) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08640.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08640.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Scaling test-time compute has proven highly effective at improving LLM reasoning. A popular method is best-of-n sampling, where a model generates many candidate solutions and one is selected. This motivates the question: how can we select the best candidate? When humans make choices, we find it more natural to compare two options than judge each in isolation. Additionally, when the options become complex, we like to judge the options one aspect at a time, such as comparing two job candidates on experience, communication, etc. In the test-time setting, candidate solutions should be judged in the same way. Here we introduce Aspect-wise Pairwise Tournaments (APT), which verifies by comparison and compares by aspect. Each matchup of a single elimination tournament is decided by a vote over multiple off-the-shelf aspect verifiers, each expressing a pairwise preference along its own aspect. Across five domains and three generator LLMs, APT improves over binary multi-aspect approval, self-consistency and reward model verification, with the largest gains on open-ended tasks. We find that both components matter; neither binary approval over the same aspects nor a generic pairwise judge at a matched budget matches APT. Because aspects are specified in natural language, APT also lets users steer selection toward properties they care about, better aligning with user preferences without needing to regenerate solutions. No component is trained, so APT transfers to new domains by simply rewriting prompts.

[arXiv:2611.08566] CascadeRec: Deployed Adaptive LLM Compute Allocation for Multi-Tenant Content Recommendation

  • arXiv ID: 2611.08566 (2611.08566v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-05
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.IR, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Kai Zhao, Guilin Zhang, Xiang Cheng, Xiquan Cui, Henry Heng, Xu Chu et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08566)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08566) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08566.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08566.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Large language models (LLMs) are attractive rerankers for recommendation. Running an expensive model on every request is costly, and for users with sparse interaction histories it often lowers quality. We present CascadeRec, a two-tier LLM reranking cascade deployed in production at a large financial planning and analysis (FP&A) SaaS provider. It serves content recommendations to enterprise tenants on AWS EKS and GKE. A lightweight Tier-1 model reranks every request. A router escalates only the requests where extra inference-time compute is likely to help, and sends them to a more expensive Tier-2 procedure (self-consistency sampling, multi-path reasoning, or a larger model). The system has run in a production A/B experiment for over a month. Compared with uniform use of the expensive tier, CascadeRec has reduced inference cost by about 10%, with minimal latency impact and no quality regression observed to date. Tenant data cannot be released, so we also evaluate the pipeline on public benchmarks (MovieLens-1M and Amazon-Electronics). Segment-level analysis with paired-bootstrap confidence intervals shows that uniform LLM reranking lowers NDCG@10 for sparse-history users by up to 30%, and its only statistically significant gain appears on the segment with the longest histories. Escalating cold users can make the loss larger instead of repairing it. Which segments benefit from escalation depends on the domain, and this motivates the segment-gated rollout we use in production. We describe the deployment architecture, the measured benefits, and lessons learned, including inter-tenant load imbalance as a driver for cascading and the handling of LLM output-parsing failures at the serving layer.

[arXiv:2611.08236] Beyond Quadratic Attention: Separable Gaussian Operators for ODE Vision Transformers

  • arXiv ID: 2611.08236 (2611.08236v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-05
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Carlos Boned Riera, David Romero Sanchez, Oriol Ramos Terrades
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08236)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08236) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08236.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08236.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Self-attention carries a quadratic cost in token count, which limits how far Vision Transformers can scale with input resolution. We introduce SSoG, a separable sum-of-Gaussians spatial operator that replaces dense self-attention with a learned mixture of axis-factorized Gaussian kernels, reducing the per-layer cost from $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$ to subquadratic in $N$. Unlike dot-product self-attention, whose Lipschitz constant is unbounded outside a compact input domain, SSoG with fixed kernels is globally Lipschitz continuous, which, via the Picard--Lindelöf theorem, guarantees that the continuous-depth variant has a unique solution on the whole integration interval, a global guarantee that dense-attention counterparts lack in general. On CIFAR-100 at $224\times224$, in a matched discrete-Transformer setting and in the continuous setting, SSoG improves Top-1 accuracy by 2.2--4.1 points across three model widths while using 8.8--16.6\% fewer parameters, with depth and training recipe held fixed. In a looped-Transformer setting, SSoG improves Top-1 accuracy by 5.4--8.1 points. We further probe inference-time scaling by evaluating on inputs up to $1024\times1024$ at matched batch size: in the continuous-depth (Neural ODE) architecture, SSoG reaches up to 154\% higher throughput and over 60\% lower peak memory than dense attention, with the gap widening as resolution grows, consistent with the difference in asymptotic cost. In the discrete and looped settings, SSoG also achieves over 30\% higher throughput than dense attention at this resolution. Together, these results establish SSoG as a practical, parameter-efficient, and theoretically grounded alternative to dense attention across discrete, weight-shared, and continuous-depth Transformer variants.

[arXiv:2611.07736] Extending Reasoning in Typed Decision Models via Looping

  • arXiv ID: 2611.07736 (2611.07736v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-05
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: David Berghaus, Arber Zela, Behzad Shomali, Markus Frey, Mehdi Ali, Rafet Sifa et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07736)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07736) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07736.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07736.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Typed decision models choose among a fixed set of answer options in a single forward pass, so unlike generative language models they cannot spend more test-time compute on hard inputs by generating more tokens. Looping a block of layers offers this compute in latent space without adding parameters, but prior work found no gain from loops retrofitted into conventionally pretrained models. We show that such loops help when trained together with the decision model. Our method, RELOAD (retrofitted loops for adaptive decisions), fine-tunes a pretrained encoder or decoder with a damped loop over its middle block and a lightweight controller picks the number of passes adaptively for each input. Across six models from 0.4B to 4B parameters, looping improves both accuracy and calibration, mostly on problems that need longer chains of reasoning than any seen in training, where encoders gain up to 23 points. Neither matched training compute nor more data closes this gap, and separate layers for each pass come close only with nearly twice the parameters. The controller keeps most of the gain at about three quarters of the compute.

[arXiv:2611.07453] Latency-Aware Adaptive Best-of-$N$

  • arXiv ID: 2611.07453 (2611.07453v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-05
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Alon Jacobson, Qian Xie, Peter I. Frazier
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07453)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07453) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07453.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07453.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Test-time compute (TTC) improves language model responses by spending additional computation at inference time, and the right amount of compute depends on the prompt. Adaptive TTC methods allocate compute based on signals of response quality such as observed rewards, often by stopping once the signal exceeds a fixed threshold, and generally measure cost by the number of generations or tokens. But in online serving, latency is also a cost: it grows with server load, so a method using a fixed threshold spends too little compute when generation is fast and too much when it is slow. For Best-of-$N$ sampling, a widely used TTC method, we propose a two-stage adaptive algorithm that draws an initial batch, then uses the observed rewards and the batch's latency to decide whether to draw a second batch and at what size by comparing expected improvement against predicted cost: it draws one when the first batch's best reward is below a threshold that decreases with the first batch's latency, without explicitly measuring server load. We map each reward to its value under the cumulative distribution function (CDF) of the prompt's reward distribution, so the same tradeoff parameters can be reused across tasks; a regression fitted offline on other prompts predicts a distribution for the CDF value of the first batch's best reward, giving the expected improvement in closed form. We evaluate the method by simulation on a preference-based alignment benchmark, measuring generations-equivalent cost: generations plus latency, converted to generations. The method reaches Best-of-$N$'s reward at lower generations-equivalent cost, and conditioning on the measured first-batch latency saves at least as much of that cost as fixed-threshold (latency-unaware) baselines, and more when latency is highly variable, when it grows with batch size, and when the second batch's latency is highly predictable from the first's.

2. [Asignación Dinámica de Cómputo de Inferencia]: Calibrar la profundidad deliberativa según la complejidad del caso: aplicar resolución directa en trámites estandarizados y expandir el árbol de búsqueda (múltiples candidatos + ranking) en casos con colisión normativa o jurisprudencial.

3. [Estructuración de Contexto y Restricciones Explícitas]: Formular las restricciones negativas (prohibición de alucinar metadatos) y las plantillas estructurales al inicio del bloque de instrucciones, utilizando precedentes reales recuperados como anclaje in-context.

4. [Búsqueda Deliberativa y Consenso Interno]: Generar y contrastar múltiples líneas argumentales (argumento principal, eventuales defensas y criterio de Alzada) seleccionando la solución que maximice la coherencia interna y la estabilidad frente a una eventual apelación.

[arXiv:2611.07367] Test-Time Self-Play: Scaling without a Verifier

  • arXiv ID: 2611.07367 (2611.07367v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-05
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Akira Yoshiyama, Akmal Ashirmatov, Jonas Hübotter, Ido Hakimi, Andreas Krause
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07367)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07367) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07367.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07367.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Test-time scaling methods spend additional compute at deployment to improve a large language model's performance on a hard target problem. Most methods rely on a verifier that scores candidate solutions for correctness, using it to select among or refine the model's outputs. In many deployment regimes, however, a strong verifier on the target problem is unavailable. We introduce Test-Time Self-Play (TTSP), an approach to test-time scaling using asymmetric self-play without reliance on a verifier. On a difficulty-calibrated subset of LiveCodeBench v6, TTSP is the best-performing method at every verifier strength we evaluate, and the gap to the strongest baseline widens as the verifier weakens; in the no-verifier limit, TTSP yields a 35\% absolute improvement in mean@8. The gains hold across a range of verifier strengths, except when the base policy has near-zero coverage of the correct answer.

[arXiv:2611.07063] SD-LoRA: Training-Time Structural Distillation into LoRA for Few-Shot Vision-Language Adaptation

  • arXiv ID: 2611.07063 (2611.07063v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-04
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Mohammed Rahman Sherif Khan Mohammad, Ardhendu Behera, Sandip Pradhan, Swagat Kumar, Yonghuai Liu
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07063)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07063) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07063.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.07063.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Few-shot adaptation of vision-language models must balance two competing goals: learning fine-grained visual distinctions from very limited supervision and preserving the efficiency that makes frozen CLIP-like models attractive for deployment. Existing lightweight adaptation methods, including prompt tuning, LoRA, and cache-based adapters, typically supervise global image embeddings and therefore underuse token-level structure. Conversely, structure-aware methods exploit local evidence but often introduce additional inference-time computation. We propose SD-LoRA, a structural distillation framework that uses token-level graph reasoning only during training and transfers its effects into a lightweight LoRA-adapted CLIP model. During training, a heterogeneous graph teacher models patch-patch, global-local, and visual-text relations over high-resolution ViT tokens. Rather than retaining this graph at test time, SD-LoRA transfers its structural bias into the shared CLIP-LoRA backbone through implicit distillation and Prototype Predictive Alignment, which aligns graph-conditioned representations with support-derived class prototypes. At inference, the graph teacher is discarded; prediction requires only a single CLIP-LoRA forward pass and a cache-based classifier. Across 11 few-shot benchmarks, SD-LoRA improves over a strong CLIP-LoRA baseline by 2.4/1.8/3.0/2.4/2.0 percentage points in the 1/2/4/8/16-shot settings, respectively, without additional graph computation at test time. Results on base-to-novel generalization, cross-dataset transfer, out-of-distribution robustness, and calibration further show that training-time structural supervision improves both accuracy and transfer reliability. Code is available at https://github.com/MR-Sherif/SD-LoRA.git.

[arXiv:2611.06868] On Turn-Level Best-of-N Sampling for Scaling LLM Agents

  • arXiv ID: 2611.06868 (2611.06868v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-04
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Jalaj Bhandari, Dhawal Gupta, Hongli Zhan, Zhenting Wang, Varad Pimpalkhute, Zhengzhong Liu et al.

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> In this work, we evaluate a simple inference-time scaling approach based on \textit{turn-level best-of-N sampling} (\method) for large language model (LLM) agents on complex, long-horizon tasks. We instantiate this using a \textit{generator-selector} framework with two LLMs; at each turn, a \emph{generator} model generates $N$ candidate actions and a \emph{selector} model selects one of them, which is then executed in the environment. This paper is closely related to the work of \cite{zainullina2025guided} who used critic models for guided search at inference time. Different from their work, we evaluate if off-the-shelf, open-weight LLMs can be effective at comparing different candidate actions directly. Experiments on long-horizon terminal tasks show that \method can lead to significant performance improvements, both in terms of accuracy (5--10\%) and consistency across repeated attempts. Analysis of agent trajectories reveal a shift in agent behaviour towards desirable patterns such as reduction in number of attempts to solve the task, improved task instruction following, debugging and testing.

[arXiv:2611.06372] Give Up to Get More: Priors, Not Policies, Govern Test-Time Compute Allocation

  • arXiv ID: 2611.06372 (2611.06372v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-04
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Jinhyung Bae
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06372)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06372) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.06372.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.06372.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> When many inputs share a budget of repeated attempts by a large language model (LLM), a common rule gives harder inputs more. On public math, code and formal-proof samples, the best published difficulty-proportional rule captures half or more of the oracle gain on benchmarks the model mostly solves, but at most 13% on hard workloads, even given true pass rates. Because the value of one more attempt peaks at intermediate difficulty, the optimal static allocation is inverted-U and gives little to inputs that are hopeless at the current budget. In a strict survey of 221 LLM allocation papers, at least 13 give the hardest inputs the most attempts; their easier settings sit at budgets where this is near-optimal, so the cost appears on harder workloads. Acting on it requires knowing which inputs are solvable, so information, not the policy, drives the gain. With a verifier and a prior from a related model's past runs on the same inputs, Thompson Sampling raises the solved share on the hardest workload from 14% to 27%; without such a prior, no policy we tested beats round-robin. For the policies we tested, the gain vanishes between prior rank correlations of about 0.3 and 0.55, which cheap text-based priors barely reach on the weakest models, and it requires a verifier whose false acceptances are rare relative to the pass rate. On new Qwen2.5 samples the pattern holds, and tempering the prior avoids losses on easy workloads. We turn these results into a decision guide.

[arXiv:2611.05919] Routes of Reasoning: Reading and Reshaping Test-Time Compute in MoE Models

  • arXiv ID: 2611.05919 (2611.05919v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-04
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Shengjie Wang, Mohsen Fayyaz, Nanyun Peng
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05919)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05919) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.05919.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.05919.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Reasoning models allocate test-time compute across distinct behaviors such as planning, deriving, and verifying. However, models frequently "overthink," continuing to verify and re-derive long after finding a correct answer. This wastes expensive compute and can even degrade performance. Existing interventions, such as token budgets or steering, primarily regulate reasoning duration rather than targeting specific behaviors. In this work, we demonstrate that Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routers provide a natural, training-free interface for behavioral control. We first split reasoning traces into sentence-level segments and label each with one of six reasoning behaviors (planning, execution, restatement, fact, reflection, and conclusion), so that every token carries the behavior of its segment. Linear probes on router logits predict this label nearly as well as probes on the residual stream, the model's full hidden state and the standard target for probing, while using about half as many features. These signals generalize: experts associated with a reasoning behavior on one dataset predict the same behavior on the others, across six datasets spanning five domains (math, code, science, knowledge, and commonsense). Building on this, we selectively edit expert router scores during inference to curb overthinking. Across four models and four benchmarks, this intervention reduces the number of generated tokens by a median of 30.4% (and up to 63.3%). Crucially, it encourages the model to stop once the problem is solved, rather than skipping essential reasoning steps. Accuracy never drops by more than 0.5 percentage points, and frequently improves. Ultimately, MoE routing offers both an interpretable lens into reasoning states and a lightweight mechanism for optimizing test-time compute.

[arXiv:2611.04663] Best-of-Depth: Learning Exploratory Dynamics in Looped Transformers

  • arXiv ID: 2611.04663 (2611.04663v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-03
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Shota Takashiro, Takeshi Kojima, Masanori Koyama, Kohei Hayashi, Yusuke Iwasawa
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04663)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04663) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.04663.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.04663.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Looped Transformers extend inference-time computation through repeated latent-state updates, but final-depth training does not directly encourage useful intermediate alternatives. We introduce Best-of-Depth (BoD), which trains a recurrent trajectory as a candidate set by minimizing prediction loss across depths. Different valid targets can be matched at different depths without architectural changes or depth-specific labels. An exact-match analysis connects this objective to solution coverage. On N-Queens completion and graph coloring, BoD improves coverage over final-depth and intermediate-supervision objectives. N-Queens coverage reaches 91.03%, versus 45.75% for final-depth training. Further experiments show discovery beyond the training depth and recovery of solutions withheld from teacher pools on familiar inputs. Fine-tuning a pretrained looped language model with BoD yields higher pooled code-generation success than final-depth training when programs are generated separately at evaluated fixed depths. These results support training recurrent computation for solution discovery along its trajectory.

[arXiv:2611.02852] Value-Conditioned Proposal Acquisition for Test-Time Reasoning

  • arXiv ID: 2611.02852 (2611.02852v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-02
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Zeyan Li, Jianfeng Xu
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02852)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02852) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02852.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02852.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Sampling and selection spend test-time compute inside a candidate pool, but they cannot recover a correct answer that never enters it. We introduce ValueFormation, a test-time policy that decides after each primary-model draw whether to stop, continue sampling, or acquire one proposal from a heterogeneous reasoning specialist. The three actions are compared on one cost-regularized value scale learned from vote-state features; the calibration is frozen across tasks and primary models, and acquired proposals are accepted only through a frozen posterior comparison. On held-out MATH-500, one Llama-fitted calibration improves over the strongest of eight identically evaluated baselines by more than fifteen points for each of three 8B primaries and transfers unchanged to Qwen3-14B. Removing acquisition lowers Llama MATH accuracy by 20.43 points, while same-trace and same-access replays attribute the gain to state-conditioned acquisition rather than specialist access alone. Where primary coverage is high, as on GSM8K, acquisition is rare and gains mainly come from cheaper allocation; on MBPP, a fixed code route attains the top accuracy at the lowest interaction cost. Varying the token price monotonically reduces calls without retraining.

Reglas Operativas Extraídas (Aplicación en Inferencia y Razonamiento)

[arXiv:2611.02366] Do Visual RL Gains Scale at Test Time? A Quantile View of Post-Training

  • arXiv ID: 2611.02366 (2611.02366v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-02
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Austin Wang, Lexing Ying
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02366)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02366) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02366.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02366.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training improves the single-sample quality of visual generative models, but deployment often provides additional inference compute through Best-of-N sampling. Whether RL gains transfer under such test-time scaling, particularly on difficult held-out tasks, remains poorly understood in the visual domain. Our central contribution is to systematically identify and characterize a failure mode of scaling transfer in visual RL: across multiple RL methods, model backbones, and evaluations spanning compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment, higher single-sample reward does not necessarily imply a better quality-compute frontier. RL gains may attenuate, saturate, or even reverse at large sampling budgets, with the base model eventually outperforming its post-trained counterpart. We explain these regimes through a quantile-transfer framework, showing that Best-of-N places increasing weight on the upper tail of the reward distribution as N grows. Consequently, RL can improve typical samples while compressing or degrading the rare high-quality outcomes that test-time scaling exploits. As a simple intervention motivated by this analysis, we introduce Quantile Utility Policy Optimization (QUPO), which optimizes a controllable mixture of quantile utilities corresponding to different inference budgets. Experiments on SD3.5-M and BAGEL show that QUPO better preserves upper-tail quality and substantially improves scaling on challenging held-out prompts relative to standard RL post-training. Our results suggest that visual generative models should be evaluated and optimized not only for single-sample quality, but for how post-training reshapes the full reward distribution that inference-time scaling exploits.

[arXiv:2611.02208] Test-Time Scaling for Open-Ended Reasoning at Small Model Scale

  • arXiv ID: 2611.02208 (2611.02208v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-01
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Adam Y. Ahmed, Abdullah G. Elafifi
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02208)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02208) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02208.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.02208.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> A vote over a model's samples can only help when the samples disagree. For open-ended generation the binding question is therefore not how to combine samples but whether a model produces samples worth combining, a question test-time scaling has answered for math but not for free-form text. We study it at small scale with semantic cluster voting, a judge-free aggregator that groups a small instruct model's samples by meaning and returns the largest cluster's representative, with no reward model or self-judging. On a 250-prompt benchmark with three tiers of answer checkability, the same pattern holds across three model families (Qwen2.5-3B, Llama-3.2-3B, Gemma-2-2B) and two generation seeds: voting raises accuracy on the math control, where samples vary but stay checkable (a gain of +10 to +29 points across the three models), yet adds at most +0.30 judge points on open-ended text. On a larger 200-problem MATH-500 run with the primary model, the math gain is +8.5 points (95% CI [+4.0, +13.0]). Almost every open-ended sample falls into one meaning cluster, so there is little to select over. The collapse is consistent with instruction-tuning rather than model size, and raising decoding temperature does not recover usable diversity, because it costs more quality than it adds (net -0.64 judge points, 95% CI [-0.93, -0.37]).

[arXiv:2611.01878] Don't Guess, Ask Wisely: Benchmarking and Training Proactive Elicitation in Open-Domain LLM Conversations

  • arXiv ID: 2611.01878 (2611.01878v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-01
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Rongyu Zhang, Mutian Bao, Jinjian Zhang, Yuetian Du, He Xu, Wei Zhou et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01878)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01878) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01878.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01878.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> When user queries are incomplete, large language models should ask targeted questions before committing to an answer, a capability we call proactive elicitation, yet evaluation on real user-assistant queries across seven LLMs (8B to frontier) shows that fewer than 11% of underspecified queries trigger a follow-up question. To benchmark and train this capability, we introduce VagueBench, where each seed query is annotated with structured intent and detail units then degraded into intent-vague and fully-vague variants via controlled removal of linguistic cues, yielding known hidden information sets for evaluating both when and what to ask. We propose DialogGRPO, an offline reinforcement learning method that organizes multi-turn elicitation as a dialogue-state directed acyclic graph, where candidate questions at each node receive dense turn-level rewards combining immediate information gain with lookahead value through group-relative comparison, enabling fine-grained credit assignment without runtime simulation. DialogGRPO elicits missing information on over 88% of vague queries with substantial gains in both recovery efficiency and answer coverage, without over-asking on complete queries, outperforms prompting and test-time-scaling baselines, and generalizes zero-shot to MultiWOZ 2.4 and AmbigQA.

[arXiv:2611.01467] See, Think, Use: Diagnosing Perception and Reasoning Failures in VLMs

  • arXiv ID: 2611.01467 (2611.01467v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-01
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Jiacheng Ding, Yanliang Qi, Cong Guo, Peihua Mai
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01467)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01467) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01467.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01467.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> We present See, Think, Use, a protocol that traces each answer of a vision-language model to perception, reasoning or the use of perception. The diagnosis shows a developer which stage to improve and directs test-time compute to the stage that fails. Benchmarks score the final answer, and an answer that rests on the image earns the same credit as a guess or a memorized prior. The protocol asks the model two extra questions per item and needs no access to its weights. A localization probe asks for a box around every instance and measures seeing, and a text-replacement probe answers from a description and measures thinking. Use is the rate at which the answer follows the model's own perception across counterfactual image pairs. The rate needs no answer labels, because the question and its prior are the same on both images. On two model families and six datasets the protocol shows that the knob that helps follows the task family. Answers follow the model's perception except on images that contradict a common prior, and asking the model to localize first repairs this failure when its boxes are right. Allocating compute by the measured stages comes close to the best uniform policy at lower cost and beats a random allocation of the same compute wherever a knob raises the short stage. We will release the code, the prompts and the per-question outputs.

[arXiv:2611.01133] Rethinking LLM Pruning via Test-Time Scaling

  • arXiv ID: 2611.01133 (2611.01133v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-31
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Mingyang Song, Mao Zheng
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01133)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01133) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01133.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.01133.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Structured pruning reduces the size of large language models, often at the cost of lower accuracy. Whether this decline reflects diminished reasoning capacity or greater difficulty in sampling correct solutions is less clear. We examine this question across expert, FFN and layer pruning on four mathematical and coding benchmarks, comparing original and pruned models through pass@k at matched sampling budgets. Even after substantial pruning, some models match or exceed the original model's reasoning coverage despite lower pass@1, and solve problems left unsolved in the original samples. Retaining broad coverage, however, does not mean that correct solutions remain easy to obtain. Under severe pruning, diverse generated chain-of-thought (CoT) traces can coexist with increased repetition and less reliable generation. As correct answers become harder to sample, the additional attempts can sharply increase total token use even when individual responses become shorter. This separation between coverage and sampling efficiency also clarifies expert-selection gains, as methods with markedly different pass@1 can solve the same problems at large budgets yet require different numbers of attempts to reach a common coverage target. Pruning should therefore be assessed by both the range of problems a model can still solve and the sampling cost of obtaining correct solutions.

[arXiv:2611.00616] Enhancing Language Model Reasoning with Structured Multi-Level Modeling

  • arXiv ID: 2611.00616 (2611.00616v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-31
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Siheng Xiong, Ali Payani, Faramarz Fekri
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00616)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00616) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.00616.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.00616.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Inference-time scaling enhances a model's reasoning by extending its chain-of-thought (CoT). However, existing approaches typically rely on a single policy trained with outcome-reward reinforcement learning (RL), which often suffers from long-horizon plan failures where the implicit plan drifts from valid strategies, especially for small LMs with limited capacity. To address this, we propose Multi-Level Reasoning (MLR), which reformulates long-CoT generation as a two-level stochastic process. A high-level planner generates structured step descriptors specifying both the reasoning mode and the semantic subgoal. The low-level executor then produces detailed reasoning conditioned on these descriptors, forming an alternating plan--execute loop. To maintain scalability, we adopt a minimal design where the base model serves as the low-level policy and a lightweight LoRA module implements the high-level policy. For training, we observe that outcome-reward RL provides sparse and delayed feedback for long trajectories (e.g., several thousand tokens), hindering credit assignment. We therefore introduce iterative Step-DPO, a process-level preference optimization scheme that leverages Twisted Sequential Monte Carlo (TSMC) to provide scalable stepwise supervision. This yields more effective training, improved stability, and higher accuracy. Extensive experiments on challenging math, science, and logical reasoning benchmarks show that, under the same reduced data budget (10% SFT and 5% preference relative to the DeepSeek-R1 distillation setup), MLR outperforms both SFT-based distillation and strong RL/preference-optimization baselines across multiple base models and tasks. Moreover, MLR exhibits slower performance degradation on long-horizon reasoning, demonstrating stronger robustness under extended CoT generation.

2. [Supervisión Granular por Pasos (Step-Level PRM)]: Validar secuencialmente cada considerando o nodo de decisión. Si un paso intermedio presenta incertidumbre o ambigüedad, podar esa rama de razonamiento y recalcular desde el último antecedente verificado.

3. [Asignación Dinámica de Cómputo de Inferencia]: Calibrar la profundidad deliberativa según la complejidad del caso: aplicar resolución directa en trámites estandarizados y expandir el árbol de búsqueda (múltiples candidatos + ranking) en casos con colisión normativa o jurisprudencial.

4. [Estructuración de Contexto y Restricciones Explícitas]: Formular las restricciones negativas (prohibición de alucinar metadatos) y las plantillas estructurales al inicio del bloque de instrucciones, utilizando precedentes reales recuperados como anclaje in-context.

[arXiv:2611.00555] Distinguishing among reasoning levels of GPT 5.5 Pro via a grammar lens benchmark

  • arXiv ID: 2611.00555 (2611.00555v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-31
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Emma Graham, Richard Granger
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00555)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00555) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.00555.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.00555.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Does the level of test-time processing effort affect a language model's computational capability? A formal grammar-lens benchmark method, derived from the theory of computation, generates novel data at specified levels, each successively more computationally powerful than the levels below. Using this data, we tested GPT-5.5, and GPT-5.5-Pro-Medium, -High, and -XHigh, showing incrementally increased acceptance of test data: 16, 40, 43, and 52\%, respectively, illustrating the benchmark's ability to discern fine-grained differences among models' added test-time reasoning levels. Additionally, as test-time compute effort increases, model responses diverge from human responses to the same data. This increasing disagreement may turn out to be broadly characteristic of more capable language models, introducing the potential of a general capability-versus-alignment conflict.

[arXiv:2611.00350] On the Limits of Test-Time Compute for Self-Evaluation

  • arXiv ID: 2611.00350 (2611.00350v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-30
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Anirudh Buvanesh, Khaled Rouissi, Sujin Yun, Johan Obando-Ceron, Morgane M Moss, Samuel Lavoie et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00350)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00350) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.00350.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.00350.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Self-verification and aggregation have emerged as popular approaches for bridging the gap between pass@1 and pass@N. However, increasing the number of candidates does not consistently narrow the gap between final-answer accuracy and pass@N. To understand this limitation, we use ranking error to measure how reliably models distinguish their correct generations from incorrect ones. We show that ranking error admits a bias-variance decomposition, with both components shaped by algorithmic design choices and inference compute. Across our experiments, additional compute reduces variance, while bias initially decreases before approaching a plateau. Guided by this analysis, we introduce Scorpio, a simple and general method that jointly scores randomly grouped candidates. Across math, code, and puzzle benchmarks, Scorpio improves verification, tree search, and aggregation. For verification, Scorpio improves accuracy over the strongest baseline by up to 7 percentage points while matching or exceeding that baseline's peak accuracy with 17-42% lower inference cost. For tree search, using Scorpio to score partial solutions and select the final answer improves accuracy by up to 10 percentage points over prior methods. Finally, Scorpio improves the scaling of aggregation-based methods by 11 percentage points on coding tasks.

[arXiv:2610.42149] Latent Reasoning in Continuous Space for Unified Multimodal Models

  • arXiv ID: 2610.42149 (2610.42149v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-30
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CV, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Byungwoo Jeon, Yoonwoo Jeong, Hyunseok Lee, Minsu Cho, Jinwoo Shin
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42149)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42149) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.42149.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.42149.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) achieve remarkable success on diverse multimodal understanding and generation tasks, yet they still struggle to effectively leverage language-based chain-of-thought reasoning for image generation. As a result, existing reasoning-augmented UMMs often fail to inherit the test-time scaling and fine-grained controllability observed in large language models. We attribute this failure to a fundamental modality mismatch: reasoning is performed in a discrete token space, whereas image generation operates in a continuous space. To bridge this gap, we introduce a Latent Reasoning framework in Continuous space (LARC), which alleviates the token-level bottleneck by reasoning over continuous hidden states. Specifically, the training framework consists of two stages: (i) curriculum supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and (ii) information-gain reinforcement learning (RL). First, the curriculum SFT stage gradually converts token-level reasoning into latent reasoning. Subsequently, the information-gain RL stage self-evolves latent reasoning without ground truth supervision. Experiments across diverse text-to-image generation benchmarks show that LARC consistently improves generation quality, prompt following, and compositional fidelity. Notably, LARC achieves state-of-the-art performance on GenEval with a 7%p gain over BAGEL and demonstrates clear test-time scaling behavior, outperforming token-based generation under both parallel and sequential test-time scaling strategies.

[arXiv:2610.41970] When Does Test-Time Search Help World Action Models? An Empirical Audit of Candidate Selection and Simulator Integrity

  • arXiv ID: 2610.41970 (2610.41970v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-30
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Test-Time Compute & Inference-Time Scaling
  • Autores: Jun Liu, Dajie Sun, Zhenglun Kong, Zhenjiang Fan, Peiyan Dong, Fan Yang et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41970)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41970) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.41970.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.41970.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> World-action models increasingly apply test-time scaling, spending extra inference computation on candidate selection and adaptive allocation to improve control. Such gains are credible only when candidates carry exploitable signal, nominal compute savings become real speedup, and evaluation stays reliable. We audit all three on Cosmos Policy and RoboCasa with a premise-first, pre-registered methodology. On clean data, a learned value selector is 1.66 pp worse than random; a hindsight oracle gains only 1.40 pp, with an episode-level interval spanning zero; and 82% of states show identical candidate outcomes, leaving little selection headroom. Token pruning cuts per-forward FLOPs by up to 47% but yields at most a measured 1.15$\times$ end-to-end speedup on the deployed path (1.25$\times$ on a decode-excluded path), short of a 1.5$\times$ target, and our adaptive-allocation experiment is inconclusive under a power gate. We further identify and fix a silent rendering corruption whose rate rises sharply along compute-heavy planning paths while leaving the official direct-policy result unaffected, and release a detector, fix, and scripts. These findings show why test-time scaling should be audited as a chain of premises (decision signal, realized efficiency, evaluationintegrity) rather than by success rate alone. Conclusions are scoped to the tested configuration.
