[arXiv:2610.35982] Generation and Verification: Human-Agent Collaboration for Safety-Critical Automotive Software Engineering

  • arXiv ID: 2610.35982 (2610.35982v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-26
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.SE, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Elizaveta Zinovyeva, Aiham Taleb, Nikita Kozodoi, Daniel Schleicher, Amir Mahdi Namazi, Martin Kraus et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35982)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35982) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35982.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.35982.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Research on coding agents mainly focuses on generation quality. Yet, safety-critical automotive software makes two additional demands. It prioritizes verification, since every change is reviewed, traced to a requirement, and checked against industry standards such as MISRA and AUTOSAR before it can be committed. It is also a narrow domain, written against layered architectures, embedded resource constraints, and organization-specific interface conventions that are scarce in the data general-purpose LLMs are trained on. We present a coding assistant for automotive C/C++ that addresses both demands. First, it exposes three tools to the user over the Model Context Protocol (generate, edit, and verify), allowing developers to run verify on any code on demand rather than relying on the agent to check its own output. Second, rather than using a general-purpose model, we customize the underlying LLM for the automotive domain through prompt engineering and fine-tuning. We construct a dataset from automotive production code of AUMOVIO, where each function is paired with the requirement texts and Unified Modeling Language (UML) behavior it must satisfy. In a two-week pilot on active production projects, developers report verify as the most-used tool and confirm using it on their own code and not only on generated output. Comparing model customization strategies, 75% prefer the faster prompt-engineered configuration. MISRA- and AUTOSAR-inspired pattern checkers indicate that prompt engineering achieves a lower number of violations on C, while fine-tuning shows better conformance with architecture patterns for C++.

[arXiv:2610.34965] The Target Agent Is Its Own Best Critic but a Poor Proposer: Self-Improved Red Teaming

  • arXiv ID: 2610.34965 (2610.34965v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-26
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Mintong Kang, Stephen Rawls, Melissa Kazemi Rad, Jian Ni, Bo Li
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34965)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34965) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34965.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34965.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Automatic red teaming increasingly relies on large language models (LLMs) to iteratively diagnose failed attacks and propose improved adversarial prompts or injections. Although prior work strengthens this loop through prompt engineering and search-harness design, the mechanisms underlying its two core roles, criticism and attack proposal, has not been explored. To further investigate the core roles and therefore improve red teaming effectiveness, we disentangle two design choices: whether criticism is generated by the target session itself (self-criticism) or by a separately instantiated critic (external criticism), and whether that criticism is translated into the next attack by the target session (self-proposal) or by a separate attacker (external proposal). Through our analysis, we find that self-criticism is more effective because it is grounded in the target's live execution context, including observations, constraints, and available reasoning; removing reasoning reduces downstream attack success by 6%-17%. In contrast, self-proposal is substantially less effective because awareness of the adversarial objective induces explicit refusal and non-refusal sandbagging. These findings motivate a role-separated red-teaming approach: the target session diagnoses why an attack failed, while a separately instantiated attacker generates the next proposal. On DTAP-BENCH, spanning Telecom, Workflow, CRM, and Code domains, under both direct misuse and indirect threat models, as well as multiple agent frameworks, this configuration achieves 63.2% overall attack success rate (ASR), much higher than baselines. Our results show that effective automatic red teaming depends not only on model capability and search strategy, but also on how execution context and adversarial responsibility are distributed across model sessions.

[arXiv:2610.34871] DocTalk: Enhancing Medical Knowledge Retrieval via Fine-tuned Large Language Models with Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for Robust Clinical Decision Support

  • arXiv ID: 2610.34871 (2610.34871v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-26
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CV
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Asad Gohar, Md Ariful Islam Mozumder, Moon-Il Joo, Hee-Cheol Kim
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34871)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34871) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34871.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34871.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Objective: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) in healthcare has opened new possibilities for AI-assisted clinical decision-making; however, safe and effective deployment remains challenging because of hallucination and domain misalignment. This study introduces DocTalk, a doctor-assistive question-answering (QA) system designed to generate concise, chart-grounded responses from de-identified discharge summaries. Methods: We developed a high-fidelity data generation pipeline that produces 500 clinically relevant QA pairs through sentence-level decomposition, chain-of-thought-guided question formulation, and expert review. Six open-source LLMs, including three general-purpose and three biomedical models, were evaluated before and after parameter-efficient instruction tuning using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). The framework integrates prompt engineering, in-context learning, and medically aligned evaluation to support factual and interpretable responses. Results: On DocTalk-QA, instruction tuning produced consistent improvements across lexical, semantic, and clinical fidelity metrics. The best-performing tuned model, Llama-3-Med42-8B, achieved ROUGE-L = 0.40, BERTScore-F1 = 0.76, and UMLS-F1 = 0.49. To examine external generalization beyond the DocTalk-QA dataset, the two best-performing models, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Llama-3-Med42-8B, were evaluated on PubMedQA before and after instruction tuning. Accuracy improved from 0.931 to 0.977 for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and from 0.935 to 0.943 for Llama-3-Med42-8B. Conclusion: DocTalk was deployed as a secure web-based platform for interactive EHR querying. These findings suggest that clinically grounded instruction tuning can improve medical QA performance and support practical clinical decision support.

[arXiv:2610.34722] CEPO: A General Framework for Improving Prompt Optimization in Multi-Agent Systems

  • arXiv ID: 2610.34722 (2610.34722v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-26
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Jongha Kim, Seohyun Lee, Jonathan Minho Chung, Jinsung Yoon, Hyunwoo J. Kim
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34722)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34722) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34722.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.34722.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Multi-agent systems (MAS) require both effective coordination and specialized task execution. Existing prompt optimization approaches typically couple these functions within agent-specific prompts. We introduce Coordinator-Executor Prompt Optimization (CEPO), a general framework for improving prompt optimization in MAS. CEPO separates system-level coordination from agent-level execution through a shared coordinator prompt and agent-specific executor prompts, and guides their updates according to their respective roles. This structure allows shared coordination rules to be refined consistently across agents while keeping execution instructions separately adjustable. CEPO provides the prompt structure and optimization guidance, while existing prompt optimizers perform the underlying search. Extensive experiments spanning 11 benchmarks across four domains demonstrate that applying CEPO to four existing prompt optimizers consistently improves their performance. Ablations and qualitative analyses support the proposed design, while controlled studies on a document-understanding MAS further demonstrate improved optimization budget efficiency and performance scaling with the number of agents.

> Pre-consultation medical dialogues are essential for collecting comprehensive patient information, but are costly to obtain at scale due to privacy and ethical constraints. Existing LLM-based synthesis methods often rely on prompt engineering or rigid rules, limiting their ability to balance clinical relevance with realistic patient diversity. We propose CoRPS-Dial, a controllable dual-agent framework for pre-consultation medical dialogue synthesis that integrates patient personas with guideline-aligned clinical workflow rules. [arXiv:2610.32073] Guided Zero-Shot Anomaly Explanation for Anonymized Crowd Video Monitoring: Context, Categorization, and Spatial Localization

  • arXiv ID: 2610.32073 (2610.32073v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-23
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Mulugeta Weldezgina Asres, Lei Jiao, Christian Walter Omlin
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32073)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32073) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.32073.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.32073.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Despite the promising development of deep learning for video anomaly detection (VAD), explainability (XAI) of these models remains a challenge. Explainable and privacy-protecting VAD builds transparency and trust that facilitates adoption of computer vision systems, including safety and security monitoring. However, anomaly explainability in privacy-preserving VAD systems is underexplored. In this study, we present the XAVAD approach, a unified descriptive anomaly XAI for an anonymized VAD, that integrates flexible zero-shot anonymization, anomaly type categorization, visual localization, and scene context description. We provide empirical and qualitative assessments of multifaceted explanations integrated with full-body visual anonymization for privacy protection. We adopt a multimodal CLIP embedding for videos, using improved LLM-assisted prompting and pool-scoring mechanisms for anomaly-type identification. We enhance Grad-ECLIP for spatial anomaly heatmap localization, leveraging computation and accuracy through our novel prompt-guided patching autofocus mechanism. We employ VLMs to generate context and reasoning descriptions of detected anomalies through curated prompt engineering. We have evaluated the accuracy and computational cost of XAVAD on several publicly available CCTV and video datasets. Our experiments demonstrate substantial gains of our XAVAD over state-of-the-art benchmark approaches and provide insights into privacy protection trade-offs in XAI for VAD. We further present ablation studies and assessments across diverse configurations and modeling alternatives to demonstrate the efficacy of our proposed methods. Code: https://github.com/muleina/XAVAD}{https://github.com/muleina/XAVAD .

[arXiv:2610.31972] Reproducible Evaluation as the Substrate for Safe Prompt Compression in Production Agents

  • arXiv ID: 2610.31972 (2610.31972v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-22
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Prabal Vashisht, Zheng Hu, Ying Yang
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31972)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31972) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.31972.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.31972.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Evaluating conversational agents that act on live, mutable enterprise data is hard: replayed sessions drift as backend state changes, and a benchmark built today can silently go stale as the data it describes moves on. We present an offline evaluation harness for a production conversational agent that stays reproducible despite the live data it operates on changing, and that can be regenerated on demand once it goes stale. We then use this harness as the fitness function for GEPA-based prompt optimization under a correctness gate that guards against reward hacking. Applied to a production merchant-facing agent, correctness-gated compression shrinks the agent's full prompt surface by 3.5-7.6% without regressing held-out task correctness, consistently across three independent subject-model families, and for one, correctness measurably improves.

[arXiv:2610.31062] SCOPE: Compiling Natural-Language Policies into Neurosymbolic Predicate Programs

  • arXiv ID: 2610.31062 (2610.31062v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-22
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Arnon Mazza, Elad Levi
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31062)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31062) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.31062.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.31062.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Policy enforcement for LLM agents has shifted from single-turn content moderation to compound, application-specific policies evaluated over multi-turn transcripts and tool-call trajectories. Fine-tuned guardrail models are efficient but degrade on compound policies, and while LLM-as-a-judge approaches achieve higher accuracy, they incur high inference cost and tend to produce unstable predictions. Neurosymbolic methods offer a natural alternative, decomposing the policy into narrow neural decisions composed under explicit logic. However, existing symbolic layers lack the required expressiveness and are optimized against fixed data rather than the policy itself. To address this, we introduce SCOPE, which compiles a policy into an executable program of neural predicates executed by small models, deterministic extractors, and code that composes their outputs. The program is optimized jointly with its training data, using adversarial generation of counterexamples that target where the program deviates from the policy. We evaluate SCOPE on a benchmark of real-world policy tasks against prompt optimization, task decomposition, and workflow generation methods, achieving the highest average accuracy across tasks and models. Programs optimized with the weakest executor transfer to other models without further optimization. This allows them to be deployed in production on inexpensive models, where they reach accuracy comparable to frontier reasoning models. Unlike these monolithic models, SCOPE produces substantially more stable predictions, and each verdict is interpretable through the predicate outputs that produced it. Additionally, we validate the key components of our method through an ablation study. SCOPE thus enables reliable, interpretable, and efficient policy enforcement at production scale.

[arXiv:2610.31060] Diagnosing the Explicit-to-Applied Theory of Mind Gap in LLMs

  • arXiv ID: 2610.31060 (2610.31060v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-22
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Prajvi Saxena, Sabine Janzen, Wolfgang Maass
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31060)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31060) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.31060.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.31060.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> As large language models are deployed in high-stakes roles such as therapy, education, and personal assistance, their social cognition must be assessed reliably. Current Theory of Mind (ToM) benchmarks report aggregate scores that conflate two distinct competencies: explicit ToM (recognizing what someone believes) and applied ToM (using that belief to predict behavior). Building on this distinction, we investigate the gap and its recoverability using four structurally diverse benchmarks. Aggregate reporting obscures uneven competence: on SimpleToM, Llama-3.1-8B scores 51.5 percent overall while recognizing mental states at 99.4 percent and predicting behavior at only 28.9 percent. To assess recovery, we compare structured prompting, matched in-context learning, and our proposed Belief-State Test-Time Training (BSTTT), which adapts lightweight parameters on answered support questions through action reconstruction. Across Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and GPT-oss-20B, recovery depends on the model, task, and intervention. On SimpleToM, reminders combined with BSTTT improve Llama's behavior prediction by 40.8 percentage points; reminders account for most of the overall improvement, with BSTTT adding 1.3 to 5.5 points beyond reminders across models. Contextual examples often outperform weight updates, while BSTTT remains stronger in specific model-benchmark settings. Temporal belief tracking remains difficult despite partial recovery. These findings show that aggregate ToM scores can overstate applied competence while hiding performance recoverable through targeted assistance. We recommend decomposed reporting of explicit and applied accuracy alongside intervention comparisons that establish which failures are recoverable and under what conditions.

[arXiv:2610.30690] From Clinical Judgment to Checkable Rules: Clinician-Led Guardrails for Mental-Health Chatbots

  • arXiv ID: 2610.30690 (2610.30690v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-22
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CY
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Chenhan Lyu, Kit Wislocki, Sarah Elizabeth Stoeckl, Maitreyi Sinha, Amir Rahmani
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30690)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30690) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.30690.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.30690.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Large language models (LLMs) deployed in mental-health contexts can be evaluated for safety failures, but the resulting judgments are often difficult to inspect or explain. Common approaches to improving safety rely on prompt engineering, LLM-as-a-Judge, and Constitutional AI. In this work, we describe a clinician-led alternative in which two clinicians define, finalize, and adjudicate the four safety checks of a rule base, which is formally verified for internal consistency and applied as a guardrail over LLM outputs. Here and in the title, clinician-led refers to this rule base, not to the fact extractor or the uncertainty default, which are ours. Every verdict of the resulting verification layer is recomputable from a named rule and sixteen facts. Against an LLM judge matched in model, revision, quantization, rule text, and decoding but without the layer's computed 988/911 fact, the largest disagreement class, the satisfied-rule class, is structured: the Llama-3.3-70B judge, on its primary prompt, demands a crisis resource from 55 of the 65 replies (84.6%) that already contain one, while the same model, asked as a fact extractor, reports a recognized resource present on 54 of the 55. The verification layer never raises that demand, by construction. We report with equal prominence where this judge is stronger: fewer false crisis alarms on the one reference-scored check. We also report the measured cost (a specification revision raised block-class outcomes on held-out boundary prompts of an over-refusal benchmark from 6.0% to 16.0%, chiefly by changing fact extraction), the clinicians' decision to keep our uncertainty default, and the analysis of the result. The contribution is not a safer verdict. It is a guardrail whose disagreements with the judge localize to named rules and facts and whose measured costs trace to named facts and the uncertainty default.

[arXiv:2610.29669] Conflicted Records: Typed Adversarial Contracts for Auditable LLM Output Quality Enforcement

  • arXiv ID: 2610.29669 (2610.29669v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-21
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.DB, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Kardelen Subasi, Jarosław A. Chudziak
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29669)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29669) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.29669.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.29669.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> LLM-generated records increasingly populate production information systems where they are treated as data assets subject to the same quality expectations as records from operational databases. A single such record can simultaneously satisfy and violate a quality rule: a grounded fact adjacent to a hallucination, a useful answer adjacent to a stereotype. Classical data cleaning cannot resolve this because the two judgments are incompatible over the same span; a single LLM judge cannot either, since it shares the training biases that produced the conflict. Existing interventions such as prompt engineering, chain-of-thought, and RLHF reduce surface error rates but provide no audit trail and cannot distinguish genuine remediation from blanket suppression. The question is whether a deterministic check over structured inter-agent records can catch the defects that referential checks alone miss. We present BiasGuard-D: two adversarial roles drawn from distinct model families exchange typed claim documents, a three-model quorum adjudicates by majority vote, and a combined referential-plus-semantic invariant validates the verdict. The semantic component fires on verdicts referential checks miss; removing the iterative loop drops invariant satisfaction sharply. The deliberation layer transfers from stereotype to hallucination remediation on HaluEval QA with only role-framing changes, raising right-answer recovery by 49 percentage points; the triage gate required a cross-family second auditor to maintain recall on objective hallucination. Every claim, vote, and instruction is stored as a typed record giving operators a provenance trail auditable under emerging AI regulations.

[arXiv:2610.28843] Beyond Correct Answers: How Compositional Cues Shape Visual Judgments

  • arXiv ID: 2610.28843 (2610.28843v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-20
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CV, cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Xiaoyu Ma, Zheyuan Zhang, Kaiwen Shi, Yanfang Ye
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28843)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28843) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28843.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28843.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Visual question answering requires models to assign attributes and relations to the correct objects, a problem known as visual binding. Structured prompts make these assignments explicit through labeled fields. Yet an improvement from the full prompt does not reveal which labels help or how they interact. First, we isolate field-label effects across ten models and six tasks by holding the image and claim fixed. These comparisons reveal differences that overall accuracy can hide. With a relation label present, adding object-role labels helps Qwen3 but hurts Qwen2 on Spatial. Second, we compare auxiliary hints that preserve or swap the question's role assignments against a no-hint baseline. Across two image sets, Qwen3's advantage with preserving hints mainly reflects errors caused by swapped hints. Preserving hints remain near baseline. Field order further affects the harm. Third, we test clearer hint wording and direct selection of the relation shown in the image. Clearer hints improve text matching but do not consistently improve visual judgments. In contrast, relation selection partly reduces errors for some hints in Qwen3 and GPT-4.1-mini.

[arXiv:2610.27473] Beyond the Prompt: Recovering Hidden Concepts in Text-to-Image Models

  • arXiv ID: 2610.27473 (2610.27473v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-20
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Zhengfeng Zheng, Minghong Fang
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27473)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27473) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27473.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27473.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Text-to-image models can generate visual concepts that their prompts never mention, and recent work shows that such associations can be implanted so that ordinary prompts trigger them. Auditing this behavior requires recovering the description that a prompt omits. Existing prompt optimizers are not well suited to this task. Gradient-based methods require backward access to the scoring encoder. Discrete evolutionary search treats semantically unrelated tokens as equally close. Language-model-guided search requires one inference call per candidate, which limits exploration. This paper presents EmbGA, an embedding-space genetic algorithm that recovers concepts present in a set of images but absent from the prompt that generated them. EmbGA keeps the population structure of genetic search but applies its operators in the token embedding space. Crossover interpolates between parent embeddings, and mutation moves an embedding toward a randomly drawn vocabulary embedding. Each offspring is then projected back to discrete tokens before scoring, so every evaluated candidate is a valid token sequence. We evaluate EmbGA on two benchmarks against six optimizers under two exploration budgets. EmbGA achieves the highest alignment when the exploration budget is large, recovers the most grounded tokens on natural images, and runs much faster than language-model-guided search.

[arXiv:2610.27367] Explore More, Validate Less: Efficient Reflective Prompt Optimization

  • arXiv ID: 2610.27367 (2610.27367v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-19
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Xiaofan Zhou, Lu Cheng
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27367)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27367) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27367.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27367.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Reflective prompt optimization improves instructions using task feedback, but coupling candidate generation with full-validation-based selection can make search costly and sequential. We propose a two-stage framework that decouples reflective candidate-pool construction from validation allocation. During construction, we sample multiple parents and reuse each parent's training responses across parallel reflection branches. Each revision is screened against its parent on a small training minibatch, allowing locally improved candidates to support further revisions without full validation. After construction, an exploration policy selects candidates from the frozen pool under a fixed full-validation budget, and the prompt with the highest measured validation score is returned. This separation allows candidate-pool size and validation budget to be set independently. We evaluate random selection and learned score prediction with similarity-based exploration on five benchmarks using Qwen3.5-9B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. With 100 retained candidates and 20 full validations, our framework improves over the initial solver by up to 11.22 percentage points. Learned selection improves over the initial solver in all ten comparisons, although neither selection policy consistently outperforms the other. These results support separating reflective search from budgeted validation while leaving room for stronger candidate-selection policies.

[arXiv:2610.27208] Language Guidance for Supervised Vision Training: An Empirical Study of Generalization

  • arXiv ID: 2610.27208 (2610.27208v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-19
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Shruthi Gowda, Nikita Jain, Elahe Arani, Bahram Zonooz
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27208)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27208) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27208.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27208.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Deep neural networks have achieved remarkable success on vision benchmarks, yet they continue to struggle with many generalization challenges. Supervised vision training relies on one-hot labels, which provide limited information about semantic structure and shared attributes between classes. This limited supervision can leave visual representations vulnerable to distribution shifts, spurious correlations, texture bias, adversarial perturbations, and forgetting in sequential learning settings. We study whether pretrained language models can serve as lightweight auxiliary supervision for vision training without requiring paired image-text data, prompt engineering, or contrastive objectives. Specifically, we evaluate two forms of language guidance, Explicit Language Guidance (ExLG) and Implicit Language Guidance (ImLG). We conduct a comprehensive evaluation across six generalization regimes, including in-distribution, out-of-distribution generalization, shortcut and spurious correlation resistance, texture and shape bias, adversarial robustness, and continual learning. Our analyses show that the two mechanisms have complementary strengths, with explicit guidance consistently benefiting in-distribution, low-data performance, and continual learning retention, while implicit guidance is often more useful in shortcut-sensitive settings and under stronger adversarial perturbations. Importantly, both are lightweight and add minimal parameters and training overhead. The analyses characterize when language-derived structure helps supervised vision training and provides a practical roadmap for using off-the-shelf pretrained models from another modality as auxiliary supervision.

[arXiv:2610.25700] From Black Box to Controller: Inference-Time Steering of LLM via Feature Amplification

  • arXiv ID: 2610.25700 (2610.25700v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-19
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Keqi Chen, Zekai Sun, Yuhua Wen, Tianyi Tian, Xinyang Huang, Huijun Lian et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25700)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25700) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25700.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25700.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Inference-time steering is crucial for deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) in real-world applications, where they must flexibly adjust behavior preferences. For instance, a model should dynamically adjust its caution level based on the risk of the scenario. This requirement introduces a challenge: modulating the intensity of LLM preferences. Prompt engineering can require task-specific prompt design, while fine-tuning requires additional optimization and data. These requirements can make repeated behavioral adjustments costly. To address this, we introduce NeuroSteer, a plug-and-play framework that enables flexible, quantitative adjustment of LLM preferences at inference time. NeuroSteer employs a Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) as its controller. Comparing neuron activation frequencies between positive and negative examples isolates "feature neurons" that correspond to target preferences such as politeness and positive sentiment. During inference, the activations of selected SAE features are amplified in the latent representations to steer the output distribution. Users can adjust the strength of this steering on a per-scenario or per-user basis, granting the LLM fine-grained, quantifiable trait modulation. Our experiments show that NeuroSteer can alter sentiment, toxicity, stance, and politeness at inference time. It achieves higher target-direction steering scores than the compared baselines in several settings, with task- and strength-dependent trade-offs in generation quality.

Reglas Operativas Extraídas (Aplicación en Inferencia y Razonamiento)

1. [Directiva de Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization]: Anclar el contexto mediante restricciones explícitas de formato, ejemplos verificados en contexto (few-shot grounding), delimitación estricta de fuentes autorizadas y descomposición modular de instrucciones.

2. [Asignación Dinámica de Cómputo de Inferencia]: Calibrar la profundidad deliberativa según la complejidad del caso: aplicar resolución directa en trámites estandarizados y expandir el árbol de búsqueda (múltiples candidatos + ranking) en casos con colisión normativa o jurisprudencial.

3. [Estructuración de Contexto y Restricciones Explícitas]: Formular las restricciones negativas (prohibición de alucinar metadatos) y las plantillas estructurales al inicio del bloque de instrucciones, utilizando precedentes reales recuperados como anclaje in-context.

[arXiv:2610.24957] Revealing what benchmark scores hide: Jointly measuring the breadth and reliability of LLM capabilities using accessibility profiles

  • arXiv ID: 2610.24957 (2610.24957v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-18
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Kazem Faghih, Yize Cheng, Shoumik Saha, Mobina Pournemat, Armin Gerami, Soheil Feizi
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24957)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24957) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24957.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24957.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Language model benchmarks assign each question a single outcome from one fixed wording, even though the same underlying request can be expressed in many equivalent ways. We argue that the quantity of interest should therefore not be correctness on one wording, but the accessibility of an inquiry, which we define as the probability that a model succeeds across meaning-preserving formulations. The distribution of accessibility across inquiries reveals a structure that accuracy and existing best-, worst-, or average-case prompt scores collapse: how broadly a model's capabilities extend across inquiries and how dependably those capabilities can be elicited. Two models can have the same average performance while one succeeds across more inquiries unreliably and the other succeeds across fewer inquiries but more consistently, leading to opposite preferences depending on whether the task allows multiple retries until one success or when repeated consistent success is required. Across six benchmarks and more than twenty models, this tradeoff is common: for about half of the model pairs with nearly equal mean accessibility, which model looks better depends on whether one success across five formulations is enough or all five must succeed, quantities we call Access@5 and Reliable@5, respectively. For some pairs the two measures diverge significantly, by up to 10 points in opposite directions. Accessibility also reveals that scaling, reasoning, and prompt optimization can redistribute capability across inquiries in ways aggregate accuracy obscures, sometimes broadening the set of inquiries a model can succeed on while making success on them less repeatable. Accessibility turns formulation sensitivity from a nuisance into a measurable property of capability, distinguishing what a model can sometimes succeed on from what users can actually depend on.

1. [Directiva de Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization]: Anclar el contexto mediante restricciones explícitas de formato, ejemplos verificados en contexto (few-shot grounding), delimitación estricta de fuentes autorizadas y descomposición modular de instrucciones.

2. [Asignación Dinámica de Cómputo de Inferencia]: Calibrar la profundidad deliberativa según la complejidad del caso: aplicar resolución directa en trámites estandarizados y expandir el árbol de búsqueda (múltiples candidatos + ranking) en casos con colisión normativa o jurisprudencial.

3. [Estructuración de Contexto y Restricciones Explícitas]: Formular las restricciones negativas (prohibición de alucinar metadatos) y las plantillas estructurales al inicio del bloque de instrucciones, utilizando precedentes reales recuperados como anclaje in-context.

[arXiv:2610.24928] Means-Ends Analysis Helps LLMs Plan in Continuous-State Domains

  • arXiv ID: 2610.24928 (2610.24928v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-18
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Jiayi Li, Carl Kingsford
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24928)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24928) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24928.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24928.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> LLM planning research has traditionally focused on discrete-state domains, where actions form a finite, enumerable set. Existing LLM planning frameworks largely assume actions are enumerable, modeling planning as a search process over candidate actions. However, in continuous-state domains, where actions form a continuum and cannot be enumerated, this assumption breaks down. We find a remedy in Newell and Simon's Means-Ends Analysis (MEA), a general problem-solving technique rooted in heuristics from human psychology. We develop Medaka, an MEA-inspired structured prompting framework that encourages recursive goal decomposition, algebraic derivation, and per-step state tracking. To evaluate native LLM planning abilities in continuous-state domains, we develop Liquidworld, a systematic testbed on liquid mixing. On Liquidworld, Medaka can improve performance across model families, suggesting that classical means-ends analysis elicits LLM-native reasoning abilities that are helpful for continuous-state planning.

Reglas Operativas Extraídas (Aplicación en Inferencia y Razonamiento)

1. [Directiva de Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization]: Anclar el contexto mediante restricciones explícitas de formato, ejemplos verificados en contexto (few-shot grounding), delimitación estricta de fuentes autorizadas y descomposición modular de instrucciones.

2. [Supervisión Granular por Pasos (Step-Level PRM)]: Validar secuencialmente cada considerando o nodo de decisión. Si un paso intermedio presenta incertidumbre o ambigüedad, podar esa rama de razonamiento y recalcular desde el último antecedente verificado.

3. [Descomposición Agéntica y Uso de Herramientas]: Dividir la tarea compleja en subtareas atómicas verificables (extracción de hechos -> búsqueda de precedentes -> encuadre legal -> redacción -> control de calidad), invocando herramientas externas de recuperación antes de razonar en abstracto.

4. [Estructuración de Contexto y Restricciones Explícitas]: Formular las restricciones negativas (prohibición de alucinar metadatos) y las plantillas estructurales al inicio del bloque de instrucciones, utilizando precedentes reales recuperados como anclaje in-context.

5. [Búsqueda Deliberativa y Consenso Interno]: Generar y contrastar múltiples líneas argumentales (argumento principal, eventuales defensas y criterio de Alzada) seleccionando la solución que maximice la coherencia interna y la estabilidad frente a una eventual apelación.

[arXiv:2610.24913] A Cross-Domain Evaluation of Prompting Techniques for Large Language Models

  • arXiv ID: 2610.24913 (2610.24913v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-10-17
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Prompt Engineering, Meta-Prompting & In-Context Optimization
  • Autores: Kevin Krazius, Tim Schlippe, Paul Libbrecht
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24913)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24913) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24913.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24913.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Prompt engineering has become a central mechanism for guiding the behavior of Large Language Models (LLMs) across a wide range of reasoning and language understanding tasks [1], [2]. However, existing studies frequently evaluate prompting techniques using different LLMs, datasets, or task settings, making direct comparisons across methods and domains difficult [3]. Consequently, in this paper we present a controlled cross-domain evaluation of Zero-Shot prompting, Few-Shot prompting, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), and automatic prompt optimization based on a PE2-inspired optimization pipeline [4]-[6]. All experiments were conducted with the same LLM and evaluated on six benchmark datasets covering arithmetic reasoning, logical reasoning, commonsense reasoning, and factual verification. The results show that no prompting strategy consistently outperforms the others across all domains, and pairwise statistical comparisons of the highest-performing configurations revealed no significant differences. RAG achieves the highest overall average accuracy of 78.33% when 300 examples are available for retrieval, while Few-Shot Prompting achieves comparatively high accuracies across several structured reasoning tasks. Zero-Shot Prompting remains competitive on tasks with self-contained inputs, whereas the PE2-inspired optimization approach produces stable but not systematically superior results compared to the other evaluated prompting techniques. Furthermore, increasing the number of task-specific examples does not consistently improve performance across prompting techniques, indicating strong domain and task dependency. These findings suggest that prompting techniques should be selected adaptively based on task characteristics rather than treated as universally applicable optimization strategies.

2. [Protocolo de Verificación Intrínseca]: Implementar un paso de auditoría independiente sobre cada afirmación fáctica y cita normativa/jurisprudencial antes de consolidar la respuesta final, rechazando cualquier inferencia no respaldada por evidencia documental directa.

4. [Estructuración de Contexto y Restricciones Explícitas]: Formular las restricciones negativas (prohibición de alucinar metadatos) y las plantillas estructurales al inicio del bloque de instrucciones, utilizando precedentes reales recuperados como anclaje in-context.

[arXiv:2611.16425] RMIT-ADM+S at TREC RAG 2026: Research Agents, Retriever Ablations, and Open-Weight Replication

  • arXiv ID: 2611.16425 (2611.16425v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-12
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.IR, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Oleg Zendel, Kun Ran, Chenglong Ma, Nguyen Khoi Vo, Shuai Wang
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16425)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16425) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16425.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.16425.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> We describe the RMIT-ADM+S team's participation in the TREC 2026 Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) track using the ClimbMix corpus. We submitted seven research-agent runs using proprietary OpenAI and open-weight models, exploring different retrieval strategies, writer models, and agent workflows. Pre-submission ablations identified writer selection and adjacent-chunk expansion as the main factors affecting answer quality. In the official NIST evaluation, six of seven runs exceeded the track median across all three rubric/nugget collections. Our best-performing run, brief-hybrid, achieved the highest all-item coverage among our submissions and tied the track maximum (0.892) for Batch-1 vital coverage. Human side-by-side preferences differed across evaluation batches: the open-weight owa-glm5 led Batch 1 despite lower automated coverage, while sol-research-first led Batch 2. These findings highlight differences between automated coverage measures and human preferences, as well as the potential of lower-cost open-weight models for research-agent systems.

[arXiv:2611.15522] GHAgentCI: A Dataset of Coding-Agent Invocations in CI Workflow Histories

  • arXiv ID: 2611.15522 (2611.15522v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-11
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.SE, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Daniel Ogenrwot, Jorge Gonzalo Delgado Cervantes, Woojoong Kim, John Businge
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15522)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15522) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15522.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15522.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Coding agents are increasingly integrated into GitHub Actions to review pull requests, triage issues and modify code. Identifying these integrations is challenging because agentic workflows often resemble ordinary CI files, with evidence appearing in action references, shell commands and workflow conventions. We present GHAgentCI, a dataset of 15K+ workflow histories from 6K+ of 196K+ mined GitHub repositories, comprising 159K+ revisions and 205K+ agent-evidence records linked to workflow triggers and declared execution constraints. Our open-source extractor, cai-miner, detects agent-related evidence using an extensible catalogue. Manual validation of 576 stratified records confirmed 96.1% of dedicated-action matches and 98.8% of GitHub Agentic Workflow matches, with lower rates for other evidence kinds. Exploratory analyses reveal a shift toward command-line and GitHub Agentic Workflow integrations, differences in declared safeguards, and frequent removal of detected agentic workflows. GHAgentCI supports longitudinal studies of coding-agent integration into CI.

[arXiv:2611.15126] Recompute or Restore? Host-Tier KV for Multi-Model Agent Workflows on a Static-Graph NPU Serving Stack

  • arXiv ID: 2611.15126 (2611.15126v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-11
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.DC, cs.LG, cs.PF
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Sungwon Woo, Seyeon Kim
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15126)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15126) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15126.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.15126.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Agent workflows issue dependent model calls that share long context prefixes, so serving them efficiently depends on reusing the key-value (KV) cache of those prefixes. On accelerators that compile model graphs ahead of time, however, the device KV budget is fixed, and prefixes evicted under load must be recomputed. Restoring them from host memory avoids this recomputation, but the transfer occupies the same engine thread as prefill and decode, so its benefit is not guaranteed. In this paper, we study when restoring beats recomputing on a commercial static-graph neural processing unit (NPU) stack (Rebellions ATOM+, vllm-rbln 0.10.2) that serves a Qwen3-8B planner on two cards and a Qwen3-0.6B executor on one. We first show that repeated prefill dominates planner work when default serving collapses: 89.5% of the executed planner prompt tokens had been materialized before. We then build a synchronous host-DRAM tier on the runtime's public KV copy calls, together with a scheduler-aware event model whose predictions are frozen before each test cohort. Experimental results show that the host tier raises workflow service-level objective (SLO) attainment from 22.8% to 100% and from 9.4% to 99.8% in the two tested overload conditions, and endpoint confirmation on two new seeds supports conservative load gains of 11.8% (chain) and 18.2% (fork), while mixed and bursty workloads remain seed-sensitive. The frozen model predicts median and 99th-percentile (P99) latency within its 15% tolerance in all 24 common-load conditions, including the 16-18% P99 increase that the tier causes for fork workflows, but it misorders three policy contrasts and overestimates the benefit of a cheaper store path (11% predicted against 3.1% measured). The trade-off therefore depends on runtime scheduling and transfer cost as well as cache capacity.

[arXiv:2611.14581] When Evaluators of AI Agents Disagree: An Empirical Study of Native Scoring, LLM Judges, and Commit-Boundary Checks on tau2-bench

  • arXiv ID: 2611.14581 (2611.14581v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-10
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.CR
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Sungbin Im
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14581)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14581) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14581.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.14581.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Agentic workflows, in which a large language model (LLM) calls tools to read and write data, are evaluated in two ways. Benchmarks compare the final state against a gold answer. Deployed services have none; an LLM judge reads the transcript and decides. Practice treats the two as interchangeable. This paper measures whether they are. We ran 2,160 episodes in tau2-bench's airline and retail domains and recorded every database write. We scored each episode four ways: native scoring against gold state, an LLM judge, that judge given the policy document, and the boundary evaluator, a minimal rule-based check of whether a write was expected against whether one occurred in the trace. Two annotators independently labeled 120 episodes for task correctness, policy compliance, and commitment correctness. The judge repeated its verdict 98.2% of the time but agreed with native scoring on 56.2%. In 11 episodes the agent executed a forbidden write. The judge passed 10 to 11, with or without the policy or transcript. A stronger judge still passed 6. The boundary evaluator, with no model call, caught all 11; no judge significantly exceeded it on human labels. Forbidden writes occurred in 11.1% of episodes without gates. Verification, correction, and safe-termination gates did not reduce them significantly. The traces show a candidate cause: the gates' evidence digest dropped the order status and customer authentication, so valid requests were rejected. The choice of evaluator decides the conclusion; the minimal check exposes failures the judges miss. Traces, code, prompts, and labels are released.

[arXiv:2611.13776] DisasterAgent: An Evidence-Driven Agent for Verifiable Disaster Report Generation From Remote Sensing Imagery

  • arXiv ID: 2611.13776 (2611.13776v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-10
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Zhijian Li, Jiawei Song, Chao Ren, Yidan Zhang, Peijin Wang, Xian Sun et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13776)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13776) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.13776.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.13776.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Remote sensing disaster assessment is moving from simple damage classification toward comprehensive report-level analysis. In this setting, responders need not only damage descriptions but also verifiable evidence to guide response and recovery. Recent vision-language models (VLMs) and tool-augmented agents offer strong multimodal reasoning and perception, but they struggle to provide the evidence required for high-stakes verification. To address these limitations, we propose DisasterAgent, an evidence-driven agent that uses a Perception-Augmentation-Generation workflow to generate verifiable disaster reports from bitemporal optical imagery. DisasterAgent first invokes a specialized vision expert toolkit in the perception phase to build a Spatiotemporal Perception Graph (STPG), serving as a structured memory to store and organize disaster-induced changes across space and time. Evidence-based Augmentation (EA) then extracts evidence from the STPG, attaching inline citations that form a closed evidence loop from each statement to the supporting visual evidence. Extensive experiments on the DisasterM3 and RSCC benchmarks demonstrate that DisasterAgent consistently outperforms general-domain VLMs, remote sensing-specialized models, and existing remote sensing agents. Notably, compared with the original model, it improves the average score for disaster report generation on DisasterM3 by 13.5\% and ST5-SCS on RSCC by 15.34\%, providing a more accurate and verifiable solution for disaster emergency response. The code will be available at https://github.com/ZhijianLi2003/DisasterAgent.

[arXiv:2611.13196] From Process Models to Composable Agentic Workflows: Combining BPMN, Agents and Enterprise Knowledge

  • arXiv ID: 2611.13196 (2611.13196v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-09
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.MA
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Christian Fillies, Jan Fillies, Frauke Weichhardt, Gay Anne Wood-Albrecht
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13196)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13196) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.13196.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.13196.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> LLM-based agents can reason, use tools and collaborate. However, coordinating increasingly complex agentic applications becomes difficult to understand, monitor and improve. Decisions distributed across prompts, tool calls and application logic make workflows implicit. We introduce Recursive Capability Composition, enabling agents to execute business processes and expose them as reusable Agentic Tools. This paper presents a BPMN-based approach to Agentic Workflows, combining explicit orchestration with autonomous reasoning. SemTalk BPMN models are transformed into executable workflows using a LangGraph/Pregel runtime. Agentic Tasks introduce LLM reasoning at selected activities, while conventional BPMN controls process flows, deterministic decisions, system interactions and human approvals. Agentic Participants provide reusable interfaces for agent behavior or other executable processes. BPMN orchestration makes multi-agent systems more understandable and manageable. Explicit process models provide developers and operators with a shared view of workflow structures and execution progress, facilitating debugging, monitoring and optimization. Enterprise agents also need organization-specific context. Process Models and Knowledge Graphs describe activities, participants, information objects, decisions and relationships between processes. They provide structured knowledge of how an organization operates, supporting informed agent decisions and actions. Our contributions are threefold: 1. Model-Driven Execution: Transforming BPMN models into executable Agentic Workflows. 2. Recursive Capability Composition: Exposing agents and executable business processes as reusable tools for other agents. 3. Enterprise Context: Using Process Models and Ontologies to provide organization-specific knowledge for agent reasoning.

3. [Descomposición Agéntica y Uso de Herramientas]: Dividir la tarea compleja en subtareas atómicas verificables (extracción de hechos -> búsqueda de precedentes -> encuadre legal -> redacción -> control de calidad), invocando herramientas externas de recuperación antes de razonar en abstracto.

[arXiv:2611.12990] Multimodal Graph Learning: A Survey of Models, Benchmarks, and Frontiers

  • arXiv ID: 2611.12990 (2611.12990v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-08
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.LG
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Rui Xue, Tianfu Wu
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12990)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12990) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12990.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12990.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Multimodal attributed graphs (MAGs), in which nodes carry text, images, and other modalities while edges encode relations among entities, underlie e-commerce, social media, content platforms, and creative applications. Learning on MAGs has developed along two complementary lines: graph-centric methods redesign how modalities are fused, propagated, and connected within message passing, while foundation-model-centric methods pretrain transferable multimodal graph encoders or bring graph context into multimodal large language models for prediction and generation. In parallel, several benchmarks have appeared with different datasets and evaluation protocols. This survey takes an algorithm-centric and evaluation-grounded view of the field and makes three contributions. Modeling: we introduce a unified message-passing formulation and organize methods into five integration levels (feature, propagation, topology, learning, and foundation model), covering discriminative and graph-conditioned generative models; within this formulation we derive representative methods as special cases, compare their assumptions, expressiveness, and cost, and identify cross-modal sensitivity as a property that separates otherwise similar architectures. Datasets and evaluation: we review recent benchmarks, summarize their datasets and metrics, define model-independent descriptors (label homophily, average degree, clustering, and edge provenance), and map each evaluation protocol to the baselines it uses. Frontiers: we connect multimodal graph learning to AI agents, covering the multimodal graphs that agents perceive and remember and graph engineering, the practice of organizing agent workflows as explicit graphs; we then discuss open problems, concrete efforts for the community, and research directions, including when graph structure helps in the presence of strong multimodal foundation models.

[arXiv:2611.12128] Toward Sustainable Agentic AI: Carbon Tradeoffs Across Iterative Self-Refinement and Tool-Augmented Coding

  • arXiv ID: 2611.12128 (2611.12128v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-09
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Boris Ruf, Adrien de Mailly Nesle, Marcin Detyniecki
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12128)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12128) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12128.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12128.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Agentic language model systems can improve performance either by iterating on their own outputs or by using external tools in grounded environments. While these strategies can reduce error rates and substitute for larger models in some settings, their environmental cost remains relatively underexplored. We present two complementary empirical studies that estimate the energy and carbon footprint of agentic AI across settings of increasing complexity. Study~1 evaluates iterative verbal self-refinement: GPT-4.1-mini at $k = 1$--$5$ steps versus a GPT-4.1 single-pass baseline on two MATH benchmark splits. Study~2 evaluates tool-augmented agentic coding: GPT-4.1-mini, Claude Opus~4.6, and GPT-5.3-codex on SWE-bench Lite, where agents operate inside Docker-isolated environments with access to code execution and file editing tools. In Study~1, $k=3$ refinements close a 16-percentage-point accuracy gap to within 1~point, but nearly triple the carbon footprint relative to the cheap single-pass baseline while remaining below the GPT-4.1 single-pass baseline under the current token-based carbon scenario. In Study~2, GPT-5.3-codex achieves the highest resolution rate in this sample (81.2\%), ahead of Claude Opus~4.6 (68.9\%) and GPT-4.1-mini (25.0\%), but resolved-task-normalized efficiency remains mixed: GPT-4.1-mini is lowest on estimated carbon and cost per resolved task, while GPT-5.3-codex delivers substantially higher task success. All carbon estimates cover output-token inference only; prompt tokens, which exceed 99\% of tokens in Study~2, and tool execution are not charged, so the figures are lower bounds on the full agentic footprint.

[arXiv:2611.12095] From Fluent Text to Ontology-Compatible Definitions: A Quality-Control Agentic Workflow

  • arXiv ID: 2611.12095 (2611.12095v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-09
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Kun Feng, Chenchen Chu, Yan Liu, Haojie Sun, Jijun Tang, Chen Tian et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12095)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12095) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12095.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.12095.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Biomedical ontologies are structured knowledge systems that must evolve continuously to represent newly emerging knowledge, but their manual maintenance remains time-consuming and laborintensive. Textual definitions are especially costly because they must be readable to humans and compatible with controlled entities, relations, and logical constraints. Large language models (LLMs) offer a promising way to assist ontology engineering tasks, including definition generation. However, textual plausibility does not guarantee knowledge-system compatibility. A generated definition may be fluent and close to a reference definition, yet still fail to ground its implied entities or violate ontology-level constraints. Directly inserting such definitions into ontology libraries may therefore introduce entity-level hallucinations and logical inconsistencies. To address this problem, we propose two structured-knowledge incompatibility metrics, Entity Error Rate (EER) and Logical Inconsistency Rate (LIR), to quantify entity grounding and logical consistency for LLM-generated ontology definitions. We further develop an agentic quality-control (QC) workflow that treats definition generation as an iterative process rather than a direct decoding step: it generates definitions, extracts axioms, checks entity existence and logical consistency, and uses the resulting diagnostics to drive repair loops. Comprehensive experiments on Disease Ontology, Uberon Ontology, and Uberon-ConstraintStress demonstrate that EER and LIR complement conventional reference-based metrics by evaluating whether generated content can be grounded and checked inside the target ontology. The workflow effectively decreases EER while maintaining strong conventional text-generation performance. LIR further exposes sparse ontology-level logical failures that are invisible to text similarity metrics, although their repair remains limited.

[arXiv:2611.10156] DeepResearch RewardBench: Evaluating Reward Models for Deep Research Report

  • arXiv ID: 2611.10156 (2611.10156v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-06
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Shudong Liu, Yuting Zhong, Guanhua Chen, Xinyi Yang, Junnan Liu, Lidia S. Chao et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10156)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10156) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10156.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.10156.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Empowered by Large Language Models (LLMs), deep research systems integrate online information into research reports through multi-step web searching and information synthesis. Evaluating these reports presents a significant challenge due to their long context, high information density, and domain specificity, necessitating the use of AI evaluation as a substitute for prohibitively expensive human assessment. However, few studies have examined the effectiveness of existing reward models or LLMs in understanding and evaluating such research reports. To bridge this gap, we introduce DeepResearch RewardBench, the first benchmark dedicated to evaluating reward modeling for research reports. We collect diverse report candidates from multiple agents or tool-augmented LLMs. These candidates are scored using query-specific golden rubrics, and we ensure data quality and difficulty through a combination of multi-round filtering and human verification. Our benchmark comprises preference datasets formatted as pairs, triplets, and quadruplets to test ranking capabilities across varying levels of difficulty. Extensive experiments demonstrate that while current reward models handle general coherence effectively, their performance significantly degrades when evaluating research reports. Generative LLMs acting as judges demonstrate comprehensive superiority in this domain, with comparative evaluation proving more effective than direct scoring methods. We anticipate that this benchmark will serve as a vital testbed for the evaluation and advancement of research agents.

[arXiv:2611.10016] Pixie: A Context-Aware Multi-Agent Multi-Modal Large Language Models (LLM) Architecture for Digital Marketing

  • arXiv ID: 2611.10016 (2611.10016v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-04
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.MA, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Vivek Vishwas Vichare, Akshat Singhal, Kirill Dubovikov, Vivek Kumar, Sreyas Chaithanya, Saurabh Verma et al.

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> We present Pixie, a context-aware, multi-agent large language model (LLM) architecture designed to enhance digital marketing operations across real-time and offline activities. Pixie uses a primary orchestration agent alongside task-specific and reconciliation/reviewer agents to support a broad range of marketing tasks. These agents combine zero-shot and few-shot prompting with marketing data, proprietary expert strategy guidelines, and documentation for proprietary statistical models. We experiment with agentic workflows ranging from single-agent to multi-agent systems. We evaluate them using assessments by trained marketing experts and measurements of LLM output variance, focusing on accuracy, consistency, and domain-specific problem-solving. Results show that Pixie's multi-agent framework improves decision quality and operational efficiency in digital marketing workflows. We assess Pixie on common digital marketing processes, including root-cause analysis and reporting, campaign research, and planning. The framework is designed to support additional specialized agents for other marketing tasks and adapt to evolving digital marketing needs.

[arXiv:2611.08452] ReproBridge: Building Reproducibility into ML Research Artifacts

  • arXiv ID: 2611.08452 (2611.08452v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-05
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Yubo Wang, Rui Meng, Jaehyun Nam, Tomas Pfister, Jinsung Yoon
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08452)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08452) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08452.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08452.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Reproducing machine learning experiments requires reconstructing procedures from information scattered across papers, released code, and supporting artifacts. For coding agents, reproduction success can therefore depend on their ability to fill in missing details, rather than on the released artifacts alone. We ask whether preserving recovered procedures and decisions improves subsequent independent reproduction. We define a standardized artifact design comprising a refined codebase, environment setup, experiment launchers, and a reproduction guide, and introduce ReproBridge, an agent workflow for constructing these refined reproducible artifacts. ReproBridge-Bench provides 1,029 tasks from 387 experiments across 36 papers, anchored by five human-verified reference scores per task. Agents attempt tasks in fresh sessions with raw code or refined artifacts; a separate Auditor checks execution evidence. Across four evaluated models, refined artifacts raise mean reproduction success from 76.4% to 92.6% and reduce downstream API spending by 19.7% on average. The cross-model success-rate spread narrows from 21.6 to 3.9 percentage points, with all four models achieving mean success rates above 90%. Removing any component reduces success in the ablation study, with the largest losses from code and environment removal. These results suggest that preserving execution knowledge helps even models with lower raw-code success reproduce experiments more reliably and at lower API cost.

[arXiv:2611.08196] SMART: State-based Multi Agent Reasoning Traces for Verification-oriented Problem-Solving

  • arXiv ID: 2611.08196 (2611.08196v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-05
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Sai Vallurupalli, Francis Ferraro
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08196)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08196) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08196.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.08196.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Complex problems with limited context, such as claims, require a means to obtain additional knowledge necessary for solution reasoning. We propose a multi-agent framework, SMART, to obtain knowledge through capturing the implicit state of participating entities in a problem context. SMART curates the state knowledge as diverse, complementary and solution-salient premises using a variety of LLMs and a structured role-based prompt strategy. Using knowledge from these real-world verified curated premises, SMART's knowledge guided solution reasoning improves validation performance up to 20% on complex scientific claims spanning 3 datasets. We show that grounding premises in real-world knowledge is necessary and improves performance up to 5%.

[arXiv:2611.06131] ModalSearcher: Weaving Visual Evidence into Autonomous Cross-Modal Deep Search

  • arXiv ID: 2611.06131 (2611.06131v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-04
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Zhihan Yin, Ruyi Li, Chengyu Fang, Yifeng Yao, Nonghai Zhang, Pengfei Xiong et al.
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06131)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06131) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.06131.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.06131.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Static and incomplete parametric knowledge limits the performance of multimodal large language models on novel-knowledge and knowledge-intensive tasks, especially those requiring multi-hop evidence acquisition. Existing multimodal agentic search methods often fix visual evidence at the input or a predefined trajectory position, making it difficult for agents to select search modalities as their information needs evolve and to handle complex real-world queries. We define this capability as autonomous cross-modal search: an agent chooses among direct reasoning, text-to-text search, text-to-image search, and reverse-image search, actively introduces visual evidence when appropriate, and switches between and integrates modalities across multiple hops. We propose ModalSearch-Pipeline, a scalable procedure for constructing an interleaved multimodal knowledge graph. It samples paths with diverse depths and modality compositions and uses backward question construction to obscure entity names and image targets that would expose reasoning shortcuts while retaining necessary disambiguating clues, yielding text-only, image-initialized, and arbitrarily interleaved multi-hop problems. We train the ModalSearcher Agent with supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning in a live Web environment, and construct ModalSearch-Bench. Experiments show that ModalSearcher-8B improves average accuracy by 16.2 points over Qwen3-VL-8B under the same agentic workflow across five multimodal knowledge-intensive QA benchmarks, with consistent gains on independently constructed active visual-search benchmarks.

Reglas Operativas Extraídas (Aplicación en Inferencia y Razonamiento)

1. [arXiv:2611.04466] From Watching to Steering: Enabling Human-Coding Agent Collaboration through Execution Traces

  • arXiv ID: 2611.04466 (2611.04466v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-03
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.HC, cs.AI
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Xiaoke Yan, Jiaying Lei, Danqing Shi, Zichen Wang, Nan Cao
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04466)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04466) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.04466.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.04466.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled coding agents to autonomously perform complex software development tasks, yet their long-running execution remains difficult for users to inspect, understand, and steer. We present Vibetrace, an interactive trace visualization and intervention system for agentic programming built upon OpenCode. Vibetrace introduces a human-agent collaboration paradigm of Observe, Understand, Intervene, and Evolve. It (1) visualizes agent workflows as interactive execution traces for inspecting actions, intermediate states, and failures; (2) enables users to fork execution from prior steps and provide targeted corrections without restarting validated progress; and (3) distills traces and human feedback into reusable skills for continual agent evolution. On SWE-bench Verified, distilled skills reduce input tokens by 23.0% and cost by 15.5%, while improving pass rate from 65.0% to 68.0%. User studies further demonstrate improved accuracy and efficiency over conventional log-based interaction.

[arXiv:2611.03223] Do Agents Still Follow the Rules? Benchmarking Rule Compliance under Agent Context Compaction

  • arXiv ID: 2611.03223 (2611.03223v1)
  • Fecha de Publicación: 2026-11-02
  • Categorías arXiv: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Eje Cognitivo: Agentic Workflows, Multi-Agent Systems & Tool-Augmented Reasoning
  • Autores: Yiwei Zha, Qingyu Meng, Herbert Bos, Min Chen
  • Enlaces Oficiales: [Abstract (https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03223)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03223) | [PDF (https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03223.pdf)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.03223.pdf)

Resumen Técnico (Technical Abstract & Mechanism)

> LLM agents compact long histories to fit their context windows, but the compacted context must still carry the operating rules that govern later actions. Prior work shows that violations rise after compaction, but measures compliance outside a complete agent harness and does not identify where each violation comes from. We introduce ARCBench(Agent Rule Compliance Benchmark), a configurable benchmark built on the open-source pi agent harness. Its tool-calling agent loop lets us measure rule compliance from actions taken during execution, bringing the evaluation closer to deployed agent workflows. Using ARCBench, we examine where violations after compaction come from, and how different compaction configurations and adversarial settings change their sources. We find that compaction rarely deletes the rule: most violations after compaction occur while the rule is still in the compacted history, with the agent treating a later request as permission, ignoring the rule, and proceeding anyway. Where violations come from depends on the configuration: how the rule is delivered decides whether the agent would break it even without compaction, while the compaction strategy and budget decide whether the rule survives. The strongest compactor-targeted attack more than doubles violations by corrupting the rule in the summary and changing how the agents behave, whereas the strongest defense restores the rule in most summary but still violates. Finally, retaining a rule does not guarantee compliance: compacted runs that retain the rule violate more than twice as often as runs with the full history.
